$k-2$ with the same data; this gives the factorization of
$C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}$ as in \eqref{eq:diag-step}. In $v'$ the value
$c=u(k-1)$ has moved to position $k$, so it passes from $\Fix$ to
$\Out$ while $d$ behaves as in (2); thus
$C^{(k-1)}_{u,v'}=\kappa\,(1+\beta y_c)^{-1}(z+\overline y_c)^{-1}C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}$.
Writing $X=(-\beta)^{s(\pi')}\beta^{t(\pi')}q^{D(\pi')}(1+\beta y_{v(\pi)(k)})^{-1}$,
the term of $(v,\pi)$ is $X(-\beta)\kappa C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}$ and the term
of $(v',\pi')$ is $X\kappa(1+\beta y_c)^{-1}(z+\overline y_c)^{-1}C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}$.
Their sum is
\[
 X\kappa\,\frac{(1+\beta y_c)^{-1}-\beta(z+\overline y_c)}{z+\overline y_c}\,
   C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}
 =X\kappa a\,C^{(k-2)}_{u,v},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:peel-residue}.
\end{proof}

The case $v(k)=u(k-1)$, in which a fixed window value would enter
position $k-1$, does not occur in a cut composite whose endpoint
satisfies $w(k)=u(k)$: it did not appear in any of the verified
ranges below, and we do not use it. The residue
\eqref{eq:peel-residue} has exactly the shape of a level-$(k-2)$ term
of \eqref{eq:junction-coeff}, up to the factor $\beta q_{k-1}$: the
missing $q_{k-1}$ appears either through $\kappa=q_{k-1}$ or through a
factor $q_{k-1}$ inside $q^{D(\pi')}$, and the missing $\beta$ through
the line path $\pi'$ being one edge shorter. This is why the junction input
is $P_{k-1}(u)+a\beta q_{k-1}P_{k-2}(u)$ and not $P_{k-1}(u)$ alone.

\begin{lemma}[Braid move]\label{lem:braid}
Let $a<k-1$. For any permutation $v$, the two-edge sequences
$(k-1,k),(a,k)$ and $(a,k-1),(k-1,k)$ from $v$ have the same endpoint
$vt_{k-1,k}t_{ak}=vt_{a,k-1}t_{k-1,k}$, and if both are directed paths
in the quantum Bruhat graph they have the same quantum weight.
Consequently, if $\gamma$ is an admissible $(k-2)$-chain to $v$ with
$\gamma\cdot(a,k-1)$ admissible, and $\pi=(k-1,k)\cdot(a,k)\cdot\pi''$
is a line path from $v$, then $(k-1,k)\cdot\pi''$ is a line path from
$vt_{a,k-1}$ with the same $v(\pi)$, and the terms indexed by
$(v,\pi)$ and $(vt_{a,k-1},(k-1,k)\cdot\pi'')$ at level $k-2$ cancel
exactly whenever the window statistics of $v$ and $vt_{a,k-1}$
agree.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The identity $t_{k-1,k}t_{ak}=t_{a,k-1}t_{k-1,k}$ holds in $S_\infty$.
For the weights write $x=v(a)$, $y=v(k-1)$, $z=v(k)$. The adjacent
transposition $(k-1,k)$ is always an edge, quantum exactly when the
larger value is on the left. If $x<y$, the edge $(a,k)$ from
$vt_{k-1,k}$ is Bruhat, and its cover condition applied to the
intermediate position $k-1$, which holds $z$, gives $z\notin(x,y)$;
hence $[y>z]=[x>z]$, and both paths have weight $q_{k-1}^{[y>z]}$.
If $x>y$, the edge $(a,k)$ from $vt_{k-1,k}$ is quantum, so
$y<z<x$, and both paths have weight $q_aq_{a+1}\cdots q_{k-1}$.
The label $(a,k-1)$ is admissible at level $k-2$ since
$a\leq k-2<k-1$, and second entries remain weakly decreasing because
$k-1$ is the least possible value. The chain gains one edge
(a factor $\beta$ when $m$ is unchanged) while the line path loses
one left edge (a factor $-\beta$), and the quantum weights agree, so
the two terms cancel.
\end{proof}

When the window statistics change in Lemma~\ref{lem:braid} (the value
$v(a)$ or $v(k-1)$ is a fixed window value), a residue of the same
kind as \eqref{eq:peel-residue} is produced instead.

\paragraph{The drop term.}
The diagonal term of \eqref{eq:junction-coeff}, $\pi=\varnothing$ and
$v=w$, contributes $(1+\beta y_{w(k)})^{-1}\bigl(C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}+a\beta q_{k-1}C^{(k-2)}_{u,w}\bigr)$,
and after \eqref{eq:diag-step} what is left of it is the drop term
in \eqref{eq:diag-cancel}. In the language of cut composites it is
the composite with an empty line path, and Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}
pairs it with the composite
$u\xrightarrow{\gamma_w}w\xrightarrow{(k-1,k)}wt_{k-1,k}\ \big|\ (k-1,k)$,
for which the move of (1) is blocked because the label $(k-1,k)$ would
be repeated. Its term is $-\beta\kappa'\,(1+\beta y_{u(k)})^{-1}C^{(k-1)}_{u,wt_{k-1,k}}$ with
$\kappa\kappa'=q_{k-1}$, and by the computation in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel} it equals
$-\beta^2q_{k-1}(1+\beta y_{u(k)})^{-1}C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}$ when
$w(k-1)\neq u(k-1)$, and
$-\beta q_{k-1}(1+\beta y_{u(k)})^{-1}(1+\beta y_{u(k-1)})^{-1}C^{(k-2)}_{u,w}
 =-\beta q_{k-1}(1+\beta y_{u(k)})^{-1}\bigl(\beta C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}+aC^{(k-2)}_{u,w}\bigr)$
when $w(k-1)=u(k-1)$, using \eqref{eq:diag-step} at level $k-1$. In
both cases it is the negative of the drop term, provided
$C^{(k-2)}_{u,w}=0$ in the first case.

\paragraph{A worked class.}
Take $u=21345$, $k=3$ and the endpoint $w=12345$, which is not in
$E(u,3)$, so the right side of \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} must vanish.
The nonzero terms are indexed by three cut composites of the single
edge sequence $(1,3),(2,3),(1,3)$ from $u$ to $w$ at level $2$, its
braid image at level $1$, and the drop:
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
 \text{level }2:& (1,3)\,\big|\,(2,3),(1,3) & \beta^2q_1q_2\,(z+\overline y_1)\,t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1},\\
 \text{level }2:& (1,3),(2,3)\,\big|\,(1,3) & -\beta q_1q_2\,t_1^{-1}t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1},\\
 \text{level }1:& (1,3)\,\big|\,(2,3),(1,3) & a\beta^3q_1q_2^2\,t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1},\\
 \text{level }1:& (1,3),(1,2)\,\big|\,(2,3) & -a\beta^3q_1q_2^2\,t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1},\\
 \text{drop}:& & a\beta q_1q_2\,t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1},
\end{array}
\]
with $t_c=1+\beta y_c$. The two level-$1$ terms are a braid pair
(Lemma~\ref{lem:braid}) and cancel. The two level-$2$ terms are a cut
sliding pair of type (3) in Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}, since the slid
value $v(2)=1=u(2)$ is fixed; their sum is the residue
$-a\beta q_1q_2t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1}$, which cancels the drop term. The
residue is absorbed by the drop rather than by a level-$1$ path term
because the relevant level-$1$ endpoint is $w$ itself.

\paragraph{Status.}
Lemmas~\ref{lem:peel} and~\ref{lem:braid} are proved. What remains
for the diagonal case is the global statement: the cut composites
with a given endpoint $w$, $w(k)=u(k)$, decompose into classes
generated by the two moves, and within each class the exact
cancellations and the residues of types \eqref{eq:peel-residue} sum
to zero against the level-$(k-2)$ terms and the drop. We have
verified this termwise, with the symbolic coefficients, for all
$u\in S_5$ and all $k$, and for a sample of $u\in S_6$ with
$k\leq5$; the identity \eqref{eq:diag-cancel} holds for every
endpoint $w$ in these ranges, including endpoints outside $E(u,k)$,
where it asserts the vanishing of the right side. A proof of the
global statement, and of its analogue for the moved cases below,
would complete the induction.

\paragraph{The moved cases.}
Suppose $u(k)\neq w(k)$. Then $C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}$ and
$C^{(k-2)}_{u,w}$ need not vanish, in contrast to the quantum
Schubert case: a $(k-1)$-chain may move position $k$ through a label
$(a,k)$. The identity \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} must then be
established by matching each $k$-chain to $w$ with a $(k-1)$- or
$(k-2)$-chain to some $v$ followed by a line path $v\to w$, and by
cancelling all remaining terms in pairs. The $k=1$ case shows the
pattern: there $P_{k-2}=0$, every nonempty line path is a single
up-path, and the matching is the identity. The complete case analysis
for $k\geq2$ is carried out below only at $q=0$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:classical}), where the matching is obtained by
counting rather than by an explicit bijection, and at $a=0$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:a-zero}).

\subsection{Special cases}
\label{sec:special}

Two specializations isolate the two difficulties of the junction
identity from each other. The first, $q=0$, is settled completely
below (Theorem~\ref{thm:classical}); Section~\ref{sec:backtrack} then
shows that its proof extends to arbitrary $q$ modulo one identity,
proves that identity for $k=2$, and Sections~\ref{sec:block}
and~\ref{sec:ybe} prove it in general.

\paragraph{The classical case \texorpdfstring{$q=0$}{q=0}.}
Set all $q_j=0$. Then all edges are Bruhat covers, the level-$(k-2)$
part of the junction input and the drop term vanish (both carry
$q_{k-1}$), and \eqref{eq:junction} reduces to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:junction-classical}
 \beta\,P_k(u)=\mathcal T_kP_{k-1}(u)-a\,P_{k-1}(u),
\end{equation}
the expansion of $\beta B_k+aB_{k-1}=(1+\beta x_k)B_{k-1}$ by the
classical equivariant $K$-Chevalley formula. The diagonal case is
then vacuous:

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:classical-diagonal}
Let $q=0$ and $w(k)=u(k)$. There is no admissible $(k-1)$-chain
$\gamma$ from $u$ to some $v$ together with a nonempty line path at
$k$ from $v$ to $w$. Consequently \eqref{eq:junction-classical} holds
at every such $w$, by \eqref{eq:diag-step}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Follow the value in position $k$ along the composite path. Every
label $(a,k)$, whether in $\gamma$ or in the left part of the line
path, is a Bruhat cover, so it replaces the value in position $k$ by
a strictly smaller one; every right edge $(k,b)$ replaces it by a
strictly larger value coming from a position $b>k$. Suppose first
that some label $(a,k)$ occurs, and let $(\alpha,k)$ be the first.
It moves $u(k)$ to position $\alpha$, which is touched afterwards
only by a left edge $(\alpha,k)$ of the line path; that edge would
have to be a Bruhat cover with $u(k)=v(\alpha)<v(k)$, whereas every
value occupying position $k$ after $(\alpha,k)$ is smaller than
$u(k)$. Hence $u(k)$ never returns to position $k$, and the right
edges, which only import values from positions beyond $k$, cannot
return it either. If no label $(a,k)$ occurs, the line path consists
of right edges only, and the first of them moves $u(k)$ to a position
$b>k$ that is never touched again. In all cases $w(k)\neq u(k)$.
\end{proof}

For the moved case at $q=0$ the computations of
Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} show that only cut sliding is needed,
together with the end braid $t_{k-1,k}t_{kb}=t_{k-1,b}t_{k-1,k}$,
which converts a right edge following a junction edge $(k-1,k)$ into
two $(k-1)$-chain labels; the residues \eqref{eq:peel-residue} cancel
the term $-aC^{(k-1)}_{u,w}$ of \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} exactly as
in the example $u=21435$, $k=3$, $w=24315$, where the pair
$u\,\big|\,(2,3),(3,4)$ and $ut_{23}\,\big|\,(3,4)$ leaves the residue
$a\beta(z+\overline y_2)t_1^{-1}=aC^{(2)}_{u,w}$.

The word-and-cut bookkeeping of Section~\ref{sec:a-zero} organizes
the classical case completely at the level of individual words. We
normalize the coefficients by
\[
 \widehat C^{(j)}_{u,v}:=\beta^{j}C^{(j)}_{u,v}
 =\beta^{s}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\phi_i(u,v;j),
 \qquad
 \phi_i=\begin{cases}
  t_{u(i)}^{-1}-a& u(i)\in\Fix,\\
  a& u(i)\in\Left,\\
  t_{u(i)}^{-1}& u(i)\in\Out,
 \end{cases}
\]
with $t_c=1+\beta y_c$; this is \eqref{eq:coefficient} at $q=0$ written
with $z+\overline y_c=(t_c^{-1}-a)/\beta$ and $m+|\Fix|=j$. In this
notation the classical junction identity \eqref{eq:junction-classical}
at the endpoint $w$ reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classical-junction-hat}
 \widehat C^{(k)}_{u,w}+a\,\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}
 =\sum_{(\gamma,\pi)\colon u\to w}
   \beta^{s(\gamma)}(-\beta)^{s(\pi)}\beta^{t(\pi)}\,
   t_{v(\pi)(k)}^{-1}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,\operatorname{end}\gamma;k-1),
\end{equation}
the sum over composites of an admissible $(k-1)$-chain $\gamma$ and a
line path $\pi$ of Bruhat edges. As in Section~\ref{sec:a-zero}, a
composite has a word
$\omega=\gamma_{>k}\,(a_1',k)\cdots(a_m',k)\,(k,b_1)\cdots(k,b_r)$ and a
cut $j$; now the chain part may contain descents, so the valid cuts
are no longer determined by monotonicity alone. Call the block entry
$a_r'$ \emph{fresh} if position $a_r'$ is touched neither by
$\gamma_{>k}$ nor by $a_1',\ldots,a_{r-1}'$, and write
$\Sigma(\omega)$ for the sum of the weights of all composites with
word $\omega$.

\begin{proposition}[Classical word lemma]\label{prop:classical-word}
Let $q=0$ and let $\omega$ be the word of a composite, with block
$(a_1',\ldots,a_m')$, and let $S$ be the length of the longest
strictly decreasing suffix $a_{m-S+1}'>\cdots>a_m'$ of the block
($S=0$ if $m=0$). Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The block entries are pairwise distinct.
\item The valid cuts form the interval $m-S\leq j\leq J$, where $J$ is
 the least index $r<m$ such that $a_r'$ is not fresh and
 $a_r'>a_{r+1}'$, or $J=m$ if there is no such $r$.
\item $\Sigma(\omega)\neq0$ if and only if $J=m$ and all entries
 $a_{m-S+1}',\ldots,a_m'$ of the decreasing suffix are fresh. In that
 case
 \[
  \Sigma(\omega)
  =\beta^{|\omega|}\,t_{c(\omega)}^{-1}\,a^{S}
   \prod_{i\notin\{a_1',\ldots,a_m'\}}\phi_i^{0}
   \prod_{r\leq m-S}\psi_r ,
 \]
 where $c(\omega)$ is the value at position $k$ after the block,
 $\phi_i^0=\phi_i(u,u\gamma_{>k};k-1)$, and for $r\leq m-S$:
 $\psi_r=\phi^0_{a_r'}$ if $a_r'$ is not fresh, $\psi_r=a$ if $a_r'$ is
 fresh and $a_r'>a_{r+1}'$, and $\psi_r=t_{u(a_r')}^{-1}$ if $a_r'$ is
 fresh and either $a_r'<a_{r+1}'$ or $r=m-S$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) A Bruhat edge $(a,k)$ requires the value at $a$ to be smaller
than the value at $k$, and it moves the smaller value to $k$; so along
the block the value at position $k$ strictly decreases. If $(a,k)$
occurred at two block indices $r<r'$, the value placed at $a$ by the
first occurrence would still be there at the second, and it exceeds
every later value at $k$; so the second occurrence is not a Bruhat
edge.

(2) By (1), an entry $a_r'$ is fresh exactly when position $a_r'$ is
not touched by $\gamma_{>k}$. The line part $(a_{j+1}',k)\cdots(a_m',k)(k,b_1)\cdots(k,b_r)$ is a
line path exactly when $a_{j+1}'>\cdots>a_m'$, that is $j\geq m-S$. The
chain part $\gamma_{>k}(a_1',k)\cdots(a_j',k)$ has distinct labels by
(1) and weakly decreasing upper indices, and condition~4 of
Definition~\ref{def:chain} is automatic inside $\gamma_{>k}$ and at
its last label. At a block index $r<j$ the lower index $a_r'$ is
repeated exactly when $a_r'$ is not fresh, since at level $k-1$ a
window position is touched only by labels with that lower index, and
then condition~4 requires $(a_r',k)\prec(a_{r+1}',k)$, that is
$a_r'<a_{r+1}'$. Hence the chain part is admissible exactly when
$j\leq J$.

(3) Fix a valid cut $j$ and let $v_j$ be the endpoint of the chain
part. We determine the status of each window position $i<k$ in $v_j$,
starting from its status in $v_0=u\gamma_{>k}$. Let $x_r$ be the value
at $a_r'$ just before $(a_r',k)$; after this label $x_r$ is at $k$, and
after $(a_{r+1}',k)$ (if $r<j$) it is at $a_{r+1}'$, where it stays,
since by (1) position $a_{r+1}'$ is not touched again by the block.
If $a_r'$ is fresh then $x_r=u(a_r')$, so position $a_r'$ has status
$\Out$ if $r=j$, status $\Left$ if $r<j$ and $a_{r+1}'<a_r'$, and status
$\Out$ if $r<j$ and $a_{r+1}'>a_r'$; before the label, for $r>j$, its
status is $\Fix$. If $a_r'$ is not fresh, then $x_r\neq u(a_r')$, and
we claim that the status of every value is unchanged by the block
labels of the chain part other than the fresh ones just described.
Indeed, a value $c=u(i)$ with status $\Left$ in $v_0$ sits at a position
$p<i$ reached by a descent $(i,b),(p,b)$ of $\gamma_{>k}$, and a chain
part containing $(p,k)$ would contain the subsequence
$(i,b),(p,b),(p,k)$ with $p<i\leq k-1<k<b$, which the prohibition
lemma \cite[Lemma~2.9]{lenart2007pieri} excludes for an admissible
chain. A value with status $\Out$ at a window position $p$ is moved
by $(p,k)$ to position $k$, and by the next label, if any, to
$a_{r+1}'$ with $a_{r+1}'>p$ by condition~4, since $p$ is a repeated
lower index; so it keeps status $\Out$. Values at positions
greater than $k$ are not moved by the block. This proves that the
weight of the composite with cut $j$ is
\[
 \beta^{|\omega|}(-1)^{m-j}\,t_{c(\omega)}^{-1}
 \prod_{i\notin\{a_r'\}}\phi_i^0
 \prod_{a_r'\text{ not fresh}}\phi^0_{a_r'}
 \prod_{a_r'\text{ fresh}}\phi_r^{(j)},
\]
where
\[
 \phi_r^{(j)}=\begin{cases}
  a& r<j,\ a_{r+1}'<a_r',\\
  t_{u(a_r')}^{-1}& r<j,\ a_{r+1}'>a_r',\ \text{or } r=j,\\
  t_{u(a_r')}^{-1}-a& r>j,
 \end{cases}
\]
the sign coming from the $m-j$ left edges of the line part.

Now sum over $j\in[m-S,J]$. If $J<m-S$ there is no valid cut and
$\Sigma(\omega)=0$; note that $J\neq m-S$ when $m-S\geq1$, since
$a_{m-S}'$ is an ascent by the maximality of $S$. For $r<m-S$ we have
$r<j$ throughout, so
$\phi^{(j)}_r=\psi_r$ is independent of $j$; for $r=m-S\geq1$, the
entry $a_{m-S}'$ is an ascent, so
$\phi^{(j)}_{m-S}=t_{u(a_{m-S}')}^{-1}$ both for $j=m-S$ and for
$j>m-S$. If $m-S<J<m$, the entries $a_r'$ with $m-S<r<J$ are fresh
descents, $a_J'$ is not fresh, and the factors for $r>J$ do not depend
on $j$. Writing $\tau_i=t_{u(a_{m-S+i}')}^{-1}$ and
$T_l=a^{l}\prod_{l<i<L+1}(\tau_i-a)$ for $0\leq l\leq L:=J-(m-S)-1$,
the remaining sum is, up to a common factor,
\begin{multline*}
 \sum_{j=m-S}^{J}(-1)^{m-j}\prod_{m-S<r<J}\phi_r^{(j)}\\
 =\pm\Bigl(T_0-(T_0+T_1)+(T_1+T_2)-\cdots
     +(-1)^{L}(T_{L-1}+T_L)+(-1)^{L+1}T_L\Bigr)=0,
\end{multline*}
because the cut $j=m-S+l$ with $1\leq l\leq L$ contributes
$a^{l-1}\tau_l\prod_{i>l}(\tau_i-a)=T_{l-1}+T_l$, the cut $j=m-S$
contributes $T_0$, and the cut $j=J$ contributes $a^L=T_L$. If $J=m$
and some suffix entry is not fresh, it must be $a_m'$ (the others are
descents), its factor $\phi^0_{a_m'}$ is constant, and the same
telescoping with $L=S-1$ gives $0$. If $J=m$ and all suffix entries
are fresh, the same computation with $L=S$, where now the cut $j=m$
contributes $a^{S-1}\tau_S=T_{S-1}+T_S$, gives $T_S=a^S$. Collecting
the constant factors yields the stated formula.
\end{proof}

Thus at $q=0$ a word contributes either nothing or a single product.
We now show that this product depends only on the endpoint $w$, not
on the word. Call a word with $\Sigma(\omega)\neq0$ \emph{surviving}.
For $j\geq0$ and arbitrary $w$ put
\[
 \Phi^{(j)}(u,w):=\beta^{\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)}
   \prod_{i=1}^{j}\phi_i(u,w;j),
\]
the endpoint factors of \eqref{eq:coefficient} at $q=0$, evaluated at
$w$ whether or not $w\in E(u,j)$. Since
$s(u,w;j)=\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)$ at $q=0$, we have
$\Phi^{(j)}(u,w)=\widehat C^{(j)}_{u,w}$ for $w\in E(u,j)$. As
$\phi_i(u,w;k)=\phi_i(u,w;k-1)$ for $i<k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-step}
 \Phi^{(k)}(u,w)=\phi_k(u,w;k)\,\Phi^{(k-1)}(u,w),
 \qquad
 \Phi^{(k)}(u,w)\big|_{y=0}
 =\beta^{\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)}(1-a)^{|\Fix(u,w;k)|}a^{|\Left(u,w;k)|}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Surviving words carry the endpoint weight]
\label{prop:survivor-weight}
Let $q=0$ and let $\omega$ be a surviving word from $u$ to $w$. If no
label of $\omega$ involves position $k$, then $w(k)=u(k)$ and
$\Sigma(\omega)=\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)+a\,\Phi^{(k-1)}(u,w)$. Otherwise
$w(k)\neq u(k)$ and $\Sigma(\omega)=\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\omega=\gamma_{>k}(a_1',k)\cdots(a_m',k)(k,b_1)\cdots(k,b_r)$,
let $v_0=u\gamma_{>k}$, and let $v$ be the permutation reached after
the block, so that $|\omega|=\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)$. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}, $J=m$, so the cut $j=m$ is
valid: $\gamma_{>k}(a_1',k)\cdots(a_m',k)$ is an admissible
$(k-1)$-chain from $u$ to $v$; and $a_m'$ is fresh when $m\geq1$. We
compare the factors in Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}(3) with
the statuses of $u(1),\ldots,u(k)$ in $w$ at level $k$.

The right edges touch only the positions $k,b_1,\ldots,b_r$, all
greater than every $i<k$. A value $u(i)$, $i<k$, sitting at one of
these positions in $v$ is in $\Out$ in $v$ at level $k-1$ and in $w$
at level $k$; any other $u(i)$ does not move. Hence
$\phi_i(u,v;k-1)=\phi_i(u,w;k)$ for all $i<k$.

Suppose $m=0$. Then $v(k)=u(k)$, so $c(\omega)=u(k)$ and
$\Sigma(\omega)=\beta^{|\omega|}t_{u(k)}^{-1}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,w;k)$.
If $r=0$ then $w=v$, $u(k)\in\Fix$, and
$t_{u(k)}^{-1}=\phi_k(u,w;k)+a$, which gives the first assertion by
\eqref{eq:Phi-step}. If $r\geq1$, the edge $(k,b_1)$ moves $u(k)$ to
position $b_1$, which is not touched again; so $u(k)\in\Out$ and
$t_{u(k)}^{-1}=\phi_k(u,w;k)$.

Suppose $m\geq1$. Position $k$ is first touched by $(a_1',k)$, which
moves $u(k)$ to $a_1'<k$, and position $a_1'$ is not touched again,
because the block entries are distinct and the right edges touch only
positions $\geq k$. So $u(k)\in\Left(u,w;k)$ and $\phi_k(u,w;k)=a$.
Since $a_m'$ is fresh, $(a_m',k)$ moves $u(a_m')$ to position $k$;
thus $c(\omega)=u(a_m')$, and $u(a_m')$ ends at $k$ or at $b_1$, so
$\phi_{a_m'}(u,w;k)=t_{u(a_m')}^{-1}$. Hence the factor
$t_{c(\omega)}^{-1}$ together with the factor $a$ that the suffix
entry $a_m'$ contributes to $a^S$ equals
$\phi_{a_m'}(u,w;k)\,\phi_k(u,w;k)$. For the remaining positions
$i<k$, $i\neq a_m'$, we show that the factor assigned to $i$ in
Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}(3) is $\phi_i(u,v;k-1)$, which
is $\phi_i(u,w;k)$. If $i=a_r'$ is fresh with $r<m$, then $u(i)$ is
moved by $(a_r',k)$ to $k$ and by $(a_{r+1}',k)$ to $a_{r+1}'$, where it
stays; the assigned factor is $a$ when $a_{r+1}'<a_r'$ (whether or not
$r$ lies in the suffix) and $t_{u(i)}^{-1}$ when $a_{r+1}'>a_r'$, in
agreement with the status $\Left$, respectively $\Out$, of $u(i)$ in
$v$. If $i$ is not a fresh block entry, the assigned factor is
$\phi_i^0=\phi_i(u,v_0;k-1)$, and the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}(3), applied to the valid cut
$j=m$, shows that the status of $u(i)$ is the same in $v_0$ and in $v$.
Multiplying all factors gives
$\Sigma(\omega)=\beta^{|\omega|}\prod_{i\leq k}\phi_i(u,w;k)
=\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)$.
\end{proof}

The proposition reduces the classical junction identity to counting
surviving words, and the count can be read off from the
non-equivariant case.

\begin{theorem}[The classical case]\label{thm:classical}
Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at $q=0$: for all $k\geq1$ and
$u\in S_\infty$,
\[
 \prod_{i=1}^k(x_i+z)\,\G_u(x;y)
 =\sum_{w\in E(u,k)}
   \beta^{\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)-m}
   \prod_{c\in\Fix}\Bigl(z-\frac{y_c}{1+\beta y_c}\Bigr)
   (1-\beta z)^{|\Left|}
   \prod_{c\in\Out}\frac1{1+\beta y_c}\;\G_w(x;y),
\]
where $E(u,k)$ is the set of endpoints of the Lenart--Sottile chains
from $u$ in the $k$-Bruhat order and $\Fix,\Left,\Out,m$ are taken
with respect to $(u,w;k)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At $q=0$ only chains without quantum edges contribute, since by
Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation}(1) no endpoint is reached by two
admissible chains; the contributing chains are the saturated chains
in the $k$-Bruhat order satisfying (P0) and (P1), with $D=0$ and
$s=\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)$. So the displayed formula is
\eqref{eq:main} at $q=0$, and as in the rest of this paragraph we
take the quantum Bruhat graph to consist of Bruhat edges only. We
induct on $k$. The case $k=1$ is
Proposition~\ref{prop:k-one}. For $k\geq2$, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:reduction} at $q=0$ it suffices to prove
\eqref{eq:junction-classical}, that is
\eqref{eq:classical-junction-hat} for every $w$. Fix $w$. If
$w(k)=u(k)$, this is Lemma~\ref{lem:classical-diagonal}. Let
$w(k)\neq u(k)$ and let $N_w$ be the number of surviving words from
$u$ to $w$, a nonnegative integer that does not depend on $y$ or $z$,
since by Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}(3) survival is a
combinatorial condition on the word. Every surviving word to $w$ has
a label involving position $k$, by Proposition~\ref{prop:survivor-weight},
so the right side of \eqref{eq:classical-junction-hat} equals
$N_w\,\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)$.

We rewrite the left side. The sets $E(u,k)$ and $E(u,k-1)$ do not both
contain $w$: along a $k$-chain the value in the window position $k$
can only increase, while along a $(k-1)$-chain the value in the
outside position $k$ can only decrease, so $w\in E(u,k)\cap E(u,k-1)$
forces $w(k)=u(k)$. If $w\in E(u,k-1)$, its chain contains a label
with upper index $k$, because $w(k)\neq u(k)$ and no other label
touches position $k$; the first one, $(\alpha,k)$, moves $u(k)$ to
position $\alpha<k$, and $u(k)$ stays there, since afterwards only
labels with upper index $k$ occur by condition~3, position $\alpha$ is
touched only by the label $(\alpha,k)$, and labels are not repeated.
Thus $u(k)\in\Left(u,w;k)$, and by
\eqref{eq:Phi-step}
$a\,\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}=a\,\Phi^{(k-1)}(u,w)=\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)$.
Therefore the left side of \eqref{eq:classical-junction-hat} equals
$c_w\,\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)$ with
$c_w=[w\in E(u,k)]+[w\in E(u,k-1)]\in\{0,1\}$, and we must show
$N_w=c_w$.

For this we specialize to $y=0$. Theorem~\ref{thm:noneq} at $q=0$
gives $B_j\G_u(x;0)=P_j(u)|_{y=0}$ for $j=k-1$ and $j=k$, where
$B_j=\prod_{i\leq j}(x_i+z)$. Together with
$\beta B_k+aB_{k-1}=X_kB_{k-1}$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:line-chevalley} at
$y=0$, this yields \eqref{eq:junction-classical} at $y=0$, hence
\eqref{eq:classical-junction-hat} at $y=0$ for our $w$:
$N_w\,\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)|_{y=0}=c_w\,\Phi^{(k)}(u,w)|_{y=0}$. By
\eqref{eq:Phi-step} the common factor is the nonzero polynomial
$\beta^{\ellength(w)-\ellength(u)}(1-a)^{|\Fix|}a^{|\Left|}$, so
$N_w=c_w$. Hence \eqref{eq:classical-junction-hat} holds for all $y$,
which completes the induction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The matching]\label{cor:matching}
Let $q=0$ and $w(k)\neq u(k)$. There is exactly one surviving word
from $u$ to $w$ if $w\in E(u,k)\cup E(u,k-1)$, and none otherwise.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}
The proof counts the surviving words without constructing them. When
$u(k)\in\Out(u,w;k)$ the surviving word is easy to describe: it is
$\gamma_w$ with its labels $(k,b)$ moved to the end. Indeed the first
label $(k,b)$ of $\gamma_w$ ends its run, as $u(k)$ would otherwise
move left; every later label $(k,b')$ ends its run by condition~4,
since $k$ is then a repeated lower index and $(k,b')\prec(a,b')$ is
impossible for $a<k$. So each $(k,b)$ commutes, as a transposition,
with all labels after it; the rearranged word has the same product
and the same length, hence is again a chain of covers, and its chain
part $\gamma_{>k}$ is admissible, since a label with a repeated lower
index that was followed by a removed $(k,b)$ is now followed by a
label with smaller upper index. When $u(k)\in\Left(u,w;k)$ the surviving word is related
to $\gamma_w$, or to the $(k-1)$-chain when $w\in E(u,k-1)$, by braid
relations such as $t_{kb}t_{ab}=t_{ak}t_{kb}$ and
$t_{ab}t_{ak}=t_{ak}t_{kb}$ as well; for $u=21435$, $k=3$, $w=43215$
the $(k-1)$-chain is $(1,4),(2,4),(1,3),(2,3)$ and the surviving word is
$(1,4)\,|\,(1,3),(2,3)\,|\,(3,4)$. We have verified
Corollary~\ref{cor:matching} and Proposition~\ref{prop:survivor-weight}
directly for all $u\in S_5$ and $k\leq5$.
\end{remark}

Theorem~\ref{thm:classical} is the classical equivariant case of
Theorem~\ref{prop:top}; together with Theorems~\ref{thm:noneq} and
\ref{thm:a-zero} it leaves open only the simultaneous presence of
$q$, $y$, and $a$. The argument has a feature worth isolating: the
word lemma shows that the $y$-dependence of every term that survives
cancellation is the same, namely that of the endpoint weight, after
which the integer multiplicity is fixed by the non-equivariant
formula. The quantum junction identity has the same shape, with
backtracks and the level-$(k-2)$ terms as additional ingredients, and
a quantum word lemma of this kind would complete the proof in the
same way.

Theorem~\ref{thm:classical} has a consequence in the $x$-variables,
which has no quantum analogue. Since $\prod_{j\leq k}(x_j+z)$ is
symmetric in $x_i,x_{i+1}$ for $i\neq k$, the isobaric divided
difference $\pi_i^x$ commutes with multiplication by it, and
$\pi^x_i\G_u=\G_{us_i}$ when $us_i<u$, $\pi^x_i\G_w=-\beta\G_w$ when
$ws_i>w$. Hence the classical coefficients satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x-recursion}
 C_{us_i,w'}
 =\begin{cases}
   C_{u,w's_i}-\beta\,C_{u,w'}& w's_i>w',\\
   0& w's_i<w',
  \end{cases}
 \qquad(us_i<u,\ i\neq k,\ q=0),
\end{equation}
a statement about the chain combinatorics that we had verified
directly for all $u\in S_4$ before Theorem~\ref{thm:classical} was
available. The identity shows where the factor $(1-\beta z)^{|\Left|}$
comes from: when a window value $c$ moves from position $i+1$ to
position $i$, the two terms on the right combine as
$(1+\beta y_c)^{-1}-\beta(z+\overline y_c)=1-\beta z$. Conversely,
\eqref{eq:x-recursion} would reduce the classical theorem by
descending induction in the right weak order to the $k$-Grassmannian
permutations $u$ (no descents outside position $k$), for which all
endpoints are again $k$-Grassmannian and the statement is an
equivariant $K$-theoretic Pieri rule for the Grassmannian,
multiplying by $\prod_{j\le k}(x_j+z)=(1-\beta z)^k\prod_j(x_j\oplus z')$
with $z'=z/(1-\beta z)$; we do not pursue this.

\paragraph{The case \texorpdfstring{$k=2$}{k=2}.}
For $k=2$ a line path has at most one left edge, namely $(1,2)$, and
the level-$0$ part of the junction input is $a\beta q_1\G_u$. The
cut composites are the sequences
\[
 (1,b_1),\ldots,(1,b_r)\ \big|\ [(1,2)]\ (2,c_1),\ldots,(2,c_t),
\]
with the junction edge $(1,2)$ possibly absent, present on one side of
the cut, or present on both sides. The last possibility is a
\emph{backtrack}: a chain ending with a quantum edge $(1,2)$ followed
by the same edge reversed as a Bruhat line edge, or vice versa. Its
contribution relative to the composite without the two edges is
$\beta\cdot(-\beta)\cdot q_1=-\beta^2q_1$, and this is exactly how the
factor $1-\beta^2q_1$ on the left of \eqref{eq:junction} is
produced. For instance, for $u=21345$ and $w=32145\in E(u,2)$, the
only terms are $u t_{13}\,\big|\,(2,3)$, with value $\beta t_1^{-1}t_2^{-1}$,
and the backtrack $u t_{13}t_{12}\,\big|\,(1,2),(2,3)$, with value
$-\beta^3q_1t_1^{-1}t_2^{-1}$, whose sum is
$\beta(1-\beta^2q_1)C^{(2)}_{u,w}$. For $w=23145$ the backtrack
$ut_{12}\,\big|\,(1,2),(2,3)$ has value $-\beta^2q_1t_1^{-1}t_2^{-1}$ and the
level-$0$ composite $u\,\big|\,(2,3)$ has value $a\beta^2q_1t_1^{-1}$;
their sum is $-\beta^3q_1(z+\overline y_2)t_1^{-1}=-\beta^2q_1\,\beta C^{(2)}_{u,w}$,
again by $(1+\beta y_2)^{-1}-a=\beta(z+\overline y_2)$. Thus for
$k=2$ all three mechanisms---cut sliding, backtracks, and the
absorption of residues by the level-$0$ terms---are visible, while
the braid move of Lemma~\ref{lem:braid} cannot occur. We have
verified \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} for $k=2$ termwise for all
$u\in S_5$. The diagonal case can be settled completely.

\begin{proposition}[Diagonal case for $k=2$]\label{prop:k2-diag}
Let $k=2$ and $w(2)=u(2)$. Then \eqref{eq:diag-cancel} holds; hence
\eqref{eq:junction-coeff} holds at $w$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We classify the nonempty cut composites ending at $w$. Position $2$
is touched by every edge of a line path at $2$. A right edge $(2,c)$
sends the value in position $2$ to the position $c>2$, which is never
touched again, so if the line path contains a right edge, the final
value in position $2$ is not $u(2)$ unless $u(2)$ was brought to
position $c$ earlier---impossible, as $c$ is touched only once. Hence
the line path is $\pi=(1,2)$, and the value $u(2)$ sits in position
$1$ of $v$, so the chain $\gamma$ to $v$ contains the label $(1,2)$.
For a level-$1$ chain this label is the last one, as $2$ is the least
admissible second entry, and $(1,2)$ moves $u(2)$ to position $1$ only
if it is the first label whose second entry is $2$; since labels are
distinct, $\gamma=\gamma_0\cdot(1,2)$ with $(1,2)\notin\gamma_0$, and
$w=vt_{12}=u\gamma_0$. Thus $\gamma_0$ is the unique $1$-chain from $u$
to $w$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:k-one}), and there is exactly one
level-$1$ composite, the backtrack $\gamma_w\cdot(1,2)\,\big|\,(1,2)$.
There is no level-$0$ composite, because a line path from $u$ cannot
restore $u(2)$ either. Write $\kappa,\kappa'$ for the weights of the
two traversals of $(1,2)$, so $\kappa\kappa'=q_1$.

If $w\neq u$, the window value $u(1)$ is in $\Out$ for both $w$ and
$wt_{12}$ (it has left position $1$ and $w(1)\neq u(1)$), so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(2) $C^{(1)}_{u,wt_{12}}=\beta\kappa C^{(1)}_{u,w}$,
and the backtrack contributes
$(-\beta)\kappa'(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}\beta\kappa C^{(1)}_{u,w}
=-\beta^2q_1(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}C^{(1)}_{u,w}$,
while the drop term is $\beta q_1(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}\beta C^{(1)}_{u,w}$
because $C^{(0)}_{u,w}=0$. They cancel.

If $w=u$, then $\gamma_0$ is empty, $C^{(1)}_{u,u}=z+\overline y_{u(1)}$,
and $u(1)$ passes from $\Fix$ to $\Out$ in $ut_{12}$, so
$C^{(1)}_{u,ut_{12}}=\kappa(1+\beta y_{u(1)})^{-1}$ and the backtrack
contributes $-\beta q_1(1+\beta y_{u(1)})^{-1}(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}$.
The drop term is
$\beta q_1(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}\bigl(\beta(z+\overline y_{u(1)})+a\bigr)
=\beta q_1(1+\beta y_{u(1)})^{-1}(1+\beta y_{u(2)})^{-1}$. They cancel.
\end{proof}

The moved case for $k=2$ requires in addition the classification of
$2$-chains: their labels form segments $(1,b)$, $(2,b)$, $(1,b)(2,b)$
or $(2,b)(1,b)$ for strictly decreasing $b$, the last form being
allowed only at the first occurrence of the lower index $2$. The
computations through $S_5$ exhibit the following pattern, which is
what a proof must establish. If $w\in E(u,2)$ and its $2$-chain has
no segment of the form $(2,b)(1,b)$, then sorting the chain, all
labels $(1,b)$ first and all labels $(2,c)$ afterwards, gives a cut
composite $\gamma^\ast\,\big|\,\pi^\ast$ whose term is
$\beta C^{(2)}_{u,w}$, and its backtrack
$\gamma^\ast(1,2)\,\big|\,(1,2)\pi^\ast$ contributes
$-\beta^3q_1C^{(2)}_{u,w}$; for instance $u=24135$, $w=31524$, with
chain $(2,5),(1,4),(2,3)$ and composite $(1,4)\,\big|\,(2,5),(2,3)$.
A segment $(2,b)(1,b)$ is where a window value moves left, and it is
resolved by $t_{2b}t_{1b}=t_{1b}t_{12}$: for $u=e$ and $w=231$, with
chain $(2,3),(1,3)$, the composites are $(1,2)\,\big|\,(2,3)$ with term
$\beta t_1^{-2}$ and $\varnothing\,\big|\,(1,2),(2,3)$ with term
$-\beta^2(z+\overline y_1)t_1^{-1}$, a cut sliding pair of type (3)
whose residue $\beta a t_1^{-1}$ is exactly $\beta C^{(2)}_{e,231}$:
the factor $1-\beta z$ attached to a value in $\Left$ is produced by
$(1+\beta y_c)^{-1}-\beta(z+\overline y_c)=1-\beta z$. The level-$0$
composite $e\,\big|\,(1,2),(2,3)$, with term $-a\beta^3q_1t_1^{-1}$,
then cancels the part $\beta^3q_1C^{(2)}_{e,231}$ of the left side.
When the $1$-chain to $w$ ends with $(1,2)$, the term
$-a(1-\beta^2q_1)C^{(1)}_{u,w}$ is cancelled by the residue of the
pair $u\,\big|\,(1,2)\pi'$, $ut_{12}\,\big|\,\pi'$ together with the
level-$0$ composite $u\,\big|\,(1,2)\pi'$, using
$t_{12}t_{2c_1}\cdots t_{2c_t}=t_{1c_1}\cdots t_{1c_t}t_{12}$ and
Postnikov's lemma for the equality of weights. The outstanding point
is the validity in the quantum Bruhat graph of the sorted and braided
sequences; Lemma~\ref{lem:sort} settles the sorting for chains
without descents, and Lemma~\ref{lem:commute} is the local step for
moving a right edge past a label with smaller upper index in general,
but the braided segments $(2,b)(1,b)$ are not covered. This case
analysis is superseded by Section~\ref{sec:backtrack}, where the case
$k=2$ is proved in full (Proposition~\ref{prop:backtrack-known}) by
discarding the right edges first.

\paragraph{The \texorpdfstring{$y$}{y}-recursion.}
Finally, there is a reduction that is valid for all $q$ and does not
use the Chevalley formula at all. The quantization map commutes with
the isobaric divided differences $\pi^y_i$ in the second alphabet,
and $\pi^y_i\G^q_w=\G^q_{s_iw}$ if $s_iw<w$, $\pi^y_i\G^q_w=-\beta\G^q_w$
otherwise. Applying $\pi^y_i$ to \eqref{eq:main} for $s_iu>u$ and
using $\pi_i(fg)=(1+\beta y_{i+1})\partial_i(f)g+s_i(f)\pi_i(g)$ shows
that the theorem for $s_iu$ implies the theorem for $u$ provided the
coefficients satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:y-recursion}
 C_{u,w}=\pi^y_i\bigl(C_{s_iu,w}\bigr)
 +\begin{cases}
   s_i\bigl(C_{s_iu,\,s_iw}\bigr)& s_iw>w,\\
   \beta\,s_i\bigl(C_{s_iu,w}\bigr)& s_iw<w.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
We have verified \eqref{eq:y-recursion} for the closed form, with
$q$ arbitrary, for all $u\in S_5$ and $k\leq3$. It reduces the
theorem for all $u\in S_n$ to the single permutation $u=w_0^{(n)}$,
for which the chains are indexed by subsets of $\{1,\ldots,k\}$ in the
classical case; the quantum case of this base is not simpler than the
general one, which is why we rely on the Chevalley induction instead.

\subsection{The general case: reduction to the backtrack identity}
\label{sec:backtrack}

We now return to the junction identity with $q$, $y$ and $a$ all
present, and show that the bootstrap of Theorem~\ref{thm:classical}
goes through verbatim once a single identity is available. That
identity involves no right edges, no endpoint set $E(u,k)$ and no
equivariant bootstrap; it is proved below for $k=2$, which completes
the proof of Theorem~\ref{prop:top} for $k=2$, it holds at $a=0$ and
at $q=0$, and it has been verified for all $u\in S_5$ and all $k$.
Section~\ref{sec:block} reduces it further, to an identity on
permutations of $k$ letters, and proves it in one of the two
resulting cases; Section~\ref{sec:ybe} proves the other case, first
when the prefix of the chain moves every position below $k-1$ and
then in general.

Throughout, $k\geq2$ and $u$ are fixed. We extend the normalization
of Section~\ref{sec:special} to arbitrary $q$: for $j\geq0$ and
$v\in E(u,j)$,
\[
 \widehat C^{(j)}_{u,v}:=\beta^{j}C^{(j)}_{u,v}
 =q^{D(u,v;j)}\beta^{s(u,v;j)}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\phi_i(u,v;j),
\]
again by $\beta(z+\overline y_c)=t_c^{-1}-a$ and $m+|\Fix|=j$, and
$\widehat C^{(j)}_{u,v}=0$ for $v\notin E(u,j)$.

\paragraph{Left and right paths.}
A line path $\pi$ at $k$ from $x$ (Definition~\ref{def:line-path}) is
the concatenation of its \emph{left path} $\lambda=(a_1,k)\cdots(a_s,k)$
and its \emph{right path} $(k,b_1)\cdots(k,b_t)$, and $v(\pi)$ is the
endpoint of $\lambda$; conversely a left path from $x$ followed by a
right path from its endpoint is a line path. A \emph{left composite}
at level $j\in\{k-1,k-2\}$ is a pair $(\gamma,\lambda)$ consisting of
an admissible $j$-chain $\gamma$ from $u$ to some $x$ and a left path
$\lambda$ at $k$ from $x$; its \emph{endpoint} is the endpoint $v$ of
$\lambda$, its \emph{word} is the label sequence $\gamma\lambda$, and
its weight is
\[
 \operatorname{wt}(\gamma,\lambda)
 :=(a\beta^2q_{k-1})^{[j=k-2]}\,
   (-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}\,t_{v(k)}^{-1}\,
   \widehat C^{(j)}_{u,x}.
\]
Let $M_{u,v}$ be the sum of the weights of all left composites with
endpoint $v$, and for a right path $\varrho$ from $v$ to $w$ let
$r_{v,w}$ be the sum of $\beta^{t(\varrho)}q^{D(\varrho)}$ over such
paths. Multiplying \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} by $\beta^{k-1}$ and
splitting every line path into its left and right paths gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:junction-split}
 (1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,\widehat C^{(k)}_{u,w}
 =\sum_{v}M_{u,v}\,r_{v,w}
  -a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{The endpoint weight.}
For arbitrary $v$ put
\[
 \Psi(u,v):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\phi_i(u,v;k)
   +[\,v(k)=u(k)\,]\;a\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\phi_i(u,v;k-1),
\]
so that $\Psi(u,v)=\prod_{i\leq k}\phi_i(u,v;k)$ if $v(k)\neq u(k)$
and $\Psi(u,v)=t_{u(k)}^{-1}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,v;k-1)$ if
$v(k)=u(k)$, because then $\phi_k(u,v;k)=t^{-1}_{u(k)}-a$. We say
that a quantity is \emph{rigid at $v$} if it equals $N\,\Psi(u,v)$
with $N$ independent of $y$. Note that
$\Psi(u,v)|_{y=0}=(1-a)^{|\Fix|}a^{|\Left|}\neq0$, where the sets are
taken at level $k$ if $v(k)\ne u(k)$ and at level $k-1$ otherwise.

\begin{lemma}[Right paths preserve the endpoint weight]
\label{lem:right-paths}
Let $v$ be the endpoint of a left composite and let $\varrho$ be a
right path at $k$ from $v$ to $w$. Then $\Psi(u,w)=\Psi(u,v)$, and
$w(k)=u(k)$ only if $\varrho$ is empty.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In $v$ the value $u(k)$ sits at a position $\leq k$: a chain at level
$k-1$ or $k-2$ and a left path touch position $k$ only through labels
$(a,k)$, which move $u(k)$ to $a<k$, and from then on $u(k)$ is moved
only by labels whose two positions are at most $k$, since in a chain
the labels with upper index greater than $k$ precede those with upper
index at most $k$. The right path touches only the positions
$k,b_1,\ldots,b_t$ with $b_i>k$, and sends the value at $k$ to $b_1$
and the value at $b_i$ to $b_{i+1}$ or, for $i=t$, to $k$. Hence if
$v(k)=u(k)$ and $\varrho\neq\varnothing$ then $w(k)\neq u(k)$, and if
$v(k)\neq u(k)$ then $u(k)$ is at a position $<k$ in both $v$ and $w$.
This proves the second assertion.

For $i<k$, let $p$ be the position of $u(i)$ in $v$. If $p<k$ it is
untouched by $\varrho$; if $p\geq k$ then $u(i)$ ends at a position
$\geq k>i$, so its status is $\Out$ in $v$ and in $w$. Thus
$\phi_i(u,v;k-1)=\phi_i(u,v;k)=\phi_i(u,w;k)$ for all $i<k$. If
$v(k)\neq u(k)$, then $u(k)$ is in $\Left$ for both $v$ and $w$ and
both weights equal $\prod_{i\leq k}\phi_i$. If $v(k)=u(k)$ and
$\varrho=\varnothing$, then $w=v$. If $v(k)=u(k)$ and
$\varrho\neq\varnothing$, then $u(k)$ ends at a position $>k$, so
$\phi_k(u,w;k)=t^{-1}_{u(k)}$ and
$\Psi(u,w)=t^{-1}_{u(k)}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,w;k)=\Psi(u,v)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Reduction to left rigidity]\label{prop:left-rigidity}
Let $k\geq2$ and suppose that Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at levels
$k-1$ and $k-2$ for $u$. If $M_{u,v}$ is rigid at every $v$, then the
junction identity \eqref{eq:junction-coeff} holds for $u$ at every
$w$, and hence Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at level $k$ for $u$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $M_{u,v}=N_v\Psi(u,v)$ with $N_v$ independent of $y$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:right-paths},
\[
 \sum_vM_{u,v}\,r_{v,w}=\widetilde N_w\,\Psi(u,w),
 \qquad \widetilde N_w=\sum_vN_v\,r_{v,w},
\]
with $\widetilde N_w$ independent of $y$. We show that the difference
of the two sides of \eqref{eq:junction-split} is a $y$-free multiple
of $\Psi(u,w)$. Since \eqref{eq:junction-split}
holds at $y=0$---Theorem~\ref{thm:noneq} at levels $k$, $k-1$, $k-2$
together with Lemma~\ref{lem:two-step} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:line-chevalley} at $y=0$ give \eqref{eq:junction} at
$y=0$, exactly as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reduction}---and $\Psi(u,w)|_{y=0}\neq0$, the
multiple is zero and \eqref{eq:junction-split} holds identically. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:reduction} this is Theorem~\ref{prop:top} at
level $k$.

Suppose $w(k)\neq u(k)$. Put
$c_j=[w\in E(u,j)]\,q^{D(u,w;j)}\beta^{s(u,w;j)}$ for $j=k,k-1$. Then
$\widehat C^{(k)}_{u,w}=c_k\prod_{i\leq k}\phi_i(u,w;k)$. If
$w\in E(u,k-1)$, the $(k-1)$-chain to $w$ contains a label with upper
index $k$, as nothing else touches position $k$; the first one,
$(\alpha,k)$, moves $u(k)$ to position $\alpha<k$, and $u(k)$ stays
there, because the later labels all have upper index $k$ and the only
one touching position $\alpha$ would be a repetition of
$(\alpha,k)$. Thus $u(k)\in\Left(u,w;k)$, $\phi_k(u,w;k)=a$, and
$a\,\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}=c_{k-1}\prod_{i\leq k}\phi_i(u,w;k)$.
Hence the difference of the two sides of \eqref{eq:junction-split} equals
\[
 \bigl((1-\beta^2q_{k-1})(c_k+c_{k-1})-\widetilde N_w\bigr)\,\Psi(u,w).
\]

Suppose $w(k)=u(k)$. By \eqref{eq:diag-step},
$\widehat C^{(k)}_{u,w}=(t^{-1}_{u(k)}-a)\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}$ and
$\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,w}=c_{k-1}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,w;k-1)$, so the
difference of the two sides of \eqref{eq:junction-split} is
$\bigl((1-\beta^2q_{k-1})c_{k-1}-\widetilde N_w\bigr)\,
 t^{-1}_{u(k)}\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,w;k-1)
 =\bigl((1-\beta^2q_{k-1})c_{k-1}-\widetilde N_w\bigr)\Psi(u,w)$.
\end{proof}

The proposition absorbs the diagonal case: \eqref{eq:diag-cancel} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:k2-diag} are no longer needed separately, since
the bootstrap handles $w(k)=u(k)$ and $w(k)\neq u(k)$ alike. It also
shows why the right edges can be ignored: they never change the
$y$-dependence.

\paragraph{Distinct left composites.}
A level-$(k-1)$ left composite $(\gamma,\lambda)$ is \emph{distinct}
if no label of $\lambda$ occurs in $\gamma$, that is, if the labels of
its word are pairwise distinct. Let $A_{u,v}$ be the sum of the
weights of the distinct left composites with endpoint $v$.

\begin{lemma}[Distinct left composites are classical]
\label{lem:distinct-rotation}
Let $\vartheta=u(k)$, $\rho=\rho_\vartheta$, and
$\sigma_j(x)=\#\{i\leq j:\ x(i)\leq\vartheta\}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For a distinct left composite with word $\omega$ and endpoint
 $v$, every edge of the rotated word is a Bruhat edge, and
 $q^{D(\omega)}=q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(u)}$.
\item Rotation is a bijection from the distinct left composites from
 $u$ onto the left composites from $\rho u$ in the classical setting
 $q=0$, and
 \[
  A_{u,v}=q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(u)}\,
   \beta^{\ellength(\rho v)-\ellength(\rho u)}\,
   n_{\rho u,\rho v}\;\Psi(u,v),
 \]
 where $n_{u',v'}$ is the number of surviving words from $u'$ to $v'$
 with empty right part, in the sense of
 Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}. In particular $A_{u,v}$ is
 rigid at every $v$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The labels of $\omega=\gamma\lambda$ satisfy $a\leq k-1<b$, are
pairwise distinct, and have weakly decreasing upper indices, since
those of $\gamma$ are at least $k$ and those of $\lambda$ equal $k$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} with $k-1$ in place of $k$ shows that every
rotated edge is Bruhat, and Lemma~\ref{lem:sigma}, with the same
substitution, gives the degree. For (2), the rotated chain is an
admissible $(k-1)$-chain of Bruhat covers from $\rho u$ by
Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation}, the rotated left path is a left path of
Bruhat covers, and conversely every classical left composite from
$\rho u$ rotates back to a distinct left composite from $u$ by
Corollary~\ref{cor:cyclic} and the distinctness of the labels. The
statuses $\Fix,\Left,\Out$ are determined by positions and are
unchanged by rotation, and $t_c$ becomes $t'_{\rho(c)}$ in the
relabelled alphabet $y'_{\rho(c)}=y_c$. Hence the weight of a distinct
left composite is $q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(u)}$ times the weight, at $q=0$
and in the alphabet $y'$, of its rotation. At $q=0$, the left
composites with a given word $\omega$ are the cuts of $\omega$ in
Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word} with $r=0$ right edges, their
total is $\Sigma(\omega)$, and by
Proposition~\ref{prop:survivor-weight} it equals
$\beta^{|\omega|}\Psi$ for surviving words and $0$ otherwise---both
cases of that proposition give $\Psi$ by its definition, and the
proofs of both propositions use only the chain and block labels,
never the right edges. Transporting back, using
$\Psi(\rho u,\rho v)(y')=\Psi(u,v)(y)$ and
$|\omega|=\ellength(\rho v)-\ellength(\rho u)$, gives the formula.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The backtrack identity.}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation}, $M_{u,v}$ is rigid if and only if
$M_{u,v}-A_{u,v}$ is, and $M_{u,v}-A_{u,v}$ is the contribution of the
level-$(k-1)$ left composites with a repeated label together with all
level-$(k-2)$ left composites. We shall prove, in
Sections~\ref{sec:block} and~\ref{sec:ybe}, that this contribution is
exactly $-\beta^2q_{k-1}A_{u,v}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:backtrack}
 M_{u,v}=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,A_{u,v}
 \qquad\text{for every }v .
\end{equation}
We call \eqref{eq:backtrack} the \emph{backtrack identity}. It is a
statement about $(k-1)$-chains, $(k-2)$-chains and left edges only.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:backtrack-implies}
Let $k\geq2$ and suppose Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at levels $k-1$
and $k-2$ for $u$. If the backtrack identity \eqref{eq:backtrack}
holds for $(u,k)$, then Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at level $k$ for
$u$. Consequently, if \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds for all $u$ and all
$k\geq2$, then Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds in general.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:backtrack} and Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation},
$M_{u,v}$ is rigid at every $v$, so
Proposition~\ref{prop:left-rigidity} applies. The second statement is
the induction on $k$, with Proposition~\ref{prop:k-one} as the base
and $P_{-1}=0$, $P_0(u)=\G^q_u$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:backtrack-known}
The backtrack identity \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds (i) for $k=2$ and
every $u$; (ii) at $a=0$; (iii) at $q=0$. Consequently
Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds for $k=2$, for all $u$ and all $q$, $y$,
$z$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(iii) At $q=0$ the level-$(k-2)$ composites vanish, both sides of
\eqref{eq:backtrack} are sums over level-$(k-1)$ left composites, and
no word has a repeated label: all block edges are Bruhat covers, so the
value at position $k$ strictly decreases along the block, and a
repeated label $(a,k)$ would return a larger value to position $k$.

(ii) At $a=0$ only Left-free chains contribute and the level-$(k-2)$
composites vanish. The analysis in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:a-zero} applies to words without right edges: a
distinct word has zero or two valid cuts, and in the latter case the
two composites cancel, so $A_{u,v}$ is the sum over the composites
$(\gamma,\varnothing)$ with $\gamma$ free of upper index $k$; a word
with a repeated label has a single valid cut and the block
\eqref{eq:single-cut-block}; those with $m\geq3$ cancel in pairs under
moving the backtrack; and the remaining ones,
$(\gamma\,(k-1,k),(k-1,k))$, have weight $-\beta^2q_{k-1}$ times the
weight of $(\gamma,\varnothing)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(2), where at
$a=0$ the correction of Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(3) is absent.

(i) Let $k=2$. The left paths at $2$ are $\varnothing$ and $(1,2)$;
a $1$-chain is $(1,b_1)\cdots(1,b_s)$ with $b_1>\cdots>b_s\geq2$, so
$(1,2)$ can only be its last label; and the only $0$-chain is empty.
Every $v$ is the endpoint of at most one $1$-chain $\gamma_v$. Fix $v$
and let $v'=vt_{12}$, with $\kappa$ and $\kappa'$ the weights of the
edges $v'\to v$ and $v\to v'$, so that $\kappa\kappa'=q_1$. The left
composites with endpoint $v$ are $(\gamma_v,\varnothing)$,
$(\gamma_{v'},(1,2))$, and, if $v\in\{u,ut_{12}\}$, the level-$0$
composites $(\varnothing,\varnothing)$ or $(\varnothing,(1,2))$. If
neither $v$ nor $v'$ is in $E(u,1)$ there is nothing to prove, so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(1) one of the following holds.

If $\gamma_v$ exists, does not contain $(1,2)$, and is nonempty, then
$\gamma_{v'}=\gamma_v(1,2)$, and $(\gamma_{v'},(1,2))$ is the only
composite with a repeated label. By Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(2),
$\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,v'}=\beta\kappa'\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,v}$, so
$M_{u,v}=t^{-1}_{v(2)}\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,v}(1-\beta^2q_1)
 =(1-\beta^2q_1)A_{u,v}$.

If $v=u$, then $\gamma_u=\varnothing$,
$\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,u}=t^{-1}_{u(1)}-a$,
$\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,ut_{12}}=\beta\kappa' t^{-1}_{u(1)}$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(3), and the three composites give
\[
 M_{u,u}=t^{-1}_{u(2)}\bigl((t^{-1}_{u(1)}-a)
   -\beta^2q_1t^{-1}_{u(1)}+a\beta^2q_1\bigr)
 =(1-\beta^2q_1)\,t^{-1}_{u(2)}(t^{-1}_{u(1)}-a)
 =(1-\beta^2q_1)A_{u,u}.
\]

If $\gamma_v=\gamma'(1,2)$ with $\gamma'\neq\varnothing$, then
$\gamma_{v'}=\gamma'$, both composites are distinct, and
$\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,v}=\beta\kappa\widehat C^{(1)}_{u,v'}$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(2) makes their weights cancel: $M_{u,v}=A_{u,v}=0$.

If $v=ut_{12}$, the composites are $((1,2),\varnothing)$,
$(\varnothing,(1,2))$ at level $1$, and $(\varnothing,(1,2))$ at
level $0$, with weights $\beta\kappa t^{-2}_{u(1)}$,
$-\beta\kappa t^{-1}_{u(1)}(t^{-1}_{u(1)}-a)$ and
$-a\beta^3q_1\kappa t^{-1}_{u(1)}$; thus
$A_{u,v}=a\beta\kappa t^{-1}_{u(1)}$ and
$M_{u,v}=(1-\beta^2q_1)A_{u,v}$.

Finally, if $v\notin E(u,1)$ but $v'\in E(u,1)$, then $\gamma_{v'}$
contains $(1,2)$, for otherwise $\gamma_{v'}(1,2)$ would be a chain to
$v$; so $v'=\operatorname{end}(\gamma'(1,2))$ and
$v=\operatorname{end}(\gamma')\in E(u,1)$, a contradiction. This
proves (i), and the last assertion follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:backtrack-implies} with Proposition~\ref{prop:k-one}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Repeated labels]\label{lem:repeat}
Let $(\gamma,\lambda)$ be a level-$(k-1)$ left composite. Every label
occurring twice in its word occurs once in $\gamma$ and once in
$\lambda$, and if some label occurs twice then $(k-1,k)$ occurs in the
word.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The labels of $\gamma$ are distinct, and so are those of $\lambda$.
Suppose the word has a repeated label, and let $(a,k)$ be the first
label of $\lambda$ that already occurs in $\gamma$, at block index $r$
of $\gamma$. The labels preceding this edge are pairwise distinct, so
by Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} (with $k-1$ in place of $k$ and
$\vartheta=u(k)$) all preceding edges are Bruhat in the rotated order.
Along a Bruhat edge $(a',k)$ the value at position $k$ is replaced by
the smaller value at $a'$, and no value at a position strictly between
$a'$ and $k$ lies between the two. Let $e$ be the value at $k$ just
before the chain label $(a,k)$ and $e'<e$ the value just after it;
then $e$ sits at position $a$ from then on, since only the label
$(a,k)$ touches position $a$. If $a=k-1$ we are done, so let $a<k-1$.
All labels between the chain label $(a,k)$ and the repeated edge have
upper index $k$, so if one of them touches position $k-1$ it is
$(k-1,k)$ and we are done. Otherwise let $c$ be the value at $k-1$,
constant along this stretch. The chain label $(a,k)$ is a Bruhat edge
with $k-1$ strictly between $a$ and $k$, so $c\notin(e',e)$. Every
subsequent edge before the repeated one has lower index $a'<k-1$ and
lowers the value at $k$ from some $f$ to some $f'<f$ with
$c\notin(f',f)$; hence, if $y$ denotes the value at $k$ just before
the repeated edge, $c\notin(y,e')$, and so $c\notin(y,e)$. But the repeated edge is a
quantum edge in the rotated order, since the value $e$ at $a$ exceeds
the value $y$ at $k$, and a quantum edge $(a,k)$ requires every value
at a position strictly between $a$ and $k$, in particular $c$, to lie
in $(y,e)$. This contradiction shows that position $k-1$ is touched,
so $(k-1,k)$ occurs.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The first family]\label{lem:first-family}
Let $(\gamma,\lambda)$ be a distinct level-$(k-1)$ left composite whose
word does not contain $(k-1,k)$. Then
$(\gamma\,(k-1,k),\ (k-1,k)\,\lambda)$ is a left composite, and:
\begin{enumerate}
\item if $\gamma$ has a label with lower index $k-1$, its weight is
 $-\beta^2q_{k-1}\operatorname{wt}(\gamma,\lambda)$;
\item otherwise $\gamma$ is a $(k-2)$-chain, $(\gamma,\lambda)$ is also
 a level-$(k-2)$ left composite, and the weights of the three
 composites $(\gamma,\lambda)$ at both levels and
 $(\gamma(k-1,k),(k-1,k)\lambda)$ add up to
 $(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\operatorname{wt}(\gamma,\lambda)$.
\end{enumerate}
Every left composite with a repeated label whose chain ends with
$(k-1,k)$, whose left path begins with $(k-1,k)$, and which has no
other repeated label arises in this way, and so does every level-$(k-2)$
left composite whose word contains neither $(k-1,k)$ nor a label of
upper index $k-1$. Consequently the backtrack identity
\eqref{eq:backtrack} holds if and only if it holds after discarding
these composites together with the distinct composites whose word does
not contain $(k-1,k)$; that is, if and only if the remaining left
composites---the distinct ones containing $(k-1,k)$, the ones with a
repeated label not of the form just described, and the level-$(k-2)$
ones containing $(k-1,k)$ or a label of upper index $k-1$---satisfy
\eqref{eq:backtrack} among themselves.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $x$ be the endpoint of $\gamma$, $x'=xt_{k-1,k}$, and let
$\kappa,\kappa'$ be the weights of the edges $x\to x'$ and $x'\to x$,
so $\kappa\kappa'=q_{k-1}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(1),
$\gamma(k-1,k)$ is an admissible $(k-1)$-chain, and $(k-1,k)\lambda$
is a left path from $x'$ because $(k-1,k)\notin\lambda$ and $k-1$ is
the largest lower index. The new composite has the same endpoint $v$,
one more chain label and one more line label, and its weight is
$-\beta\kappa'\,(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}t^{-1}_{v(k)}
 \widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,x'}$. Since $(k-1,k)\notin\gamma$, the value
$u(k-1)$ is not at position $k$ in $x$. In case (1),
$x(k-1)\neq u(k-1)$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(2) gives
$\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,x'}=\beta\kappa\,\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,x}$, whence
the claim. In case (2), $\gamma$ has no label with lower index $k-1$,
so it is an admissible $(k-2)$-chain with the same data, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(3) gives
$\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,x}=(t^{-1}_{u(k-1)}-a)\widehat C^{(k-2)}_{u,x}$ and
$\widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,x'}=\beta\kappa\,t^{-1}_{u(k-1)}\widehat C^{(k-2)}_{u,x}$.
With $X=(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}t^{-1}_{v(k)}\widehat C^{(k-2)}_{u,x}$
the three weights are $X(t^{-1}_{u(k-1)}-a)$,
$-\beta^2q_{k-1}X\,t^{-1}_{u(k-1)}$ and $a\beta^2q_{k-1}X$, whose sum is
$(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})X(t^{-1}_{u(k-1)}-a)$.

Conversely, removing the two labels $(k-1,k)$ from a composite of the
first kind gives a distinct composite with the same endpoint whose
word avoids $(k-1,k)$, and a level-$(k-2)$ composite of the second kind
is a distinct level-$(k-1)$ composite by Lemma~\ref{lem:repeat}, with
a $(k-2)$-chain as chain part. The last assertion follows, since the
composites discarded contribute $(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})$ times the
discarded part of $A_{u,v}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Evidence and structure for $k\geq3$]
\label{rem:backtrack-evidence}
We have verified \eqref{eq:backtrack} for all $u\in S_5$ and
$2\leq k\leq5$, by evaluating both sides over a prime field at random
values of $\beta,q,y,a$; by Theorem~\ref{thm:backtrack-implies} this
reproves Theorem~\ref{prop:top} in that range. The same computations
show that $M_{u,v}$ is rigid at every $v$, that $A_{u,v}$ agrees with
the rotated classical sum of Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation}, and
that \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds separately for each prefix: grouping
the left composites by the labels of their chain with upper index
greater than $k$, each group satisfies \eqref{eq:backtrack} on its own.
Thus the identity is a statement about the positions $1,\ldots,k$
alone; Section~\ref{sec:block} proves this
(Proposition~\ref{prop:block-reduction}). The composites left over by
Lemma~\ref{lem:first-family} all
involve the label $(k-1,k)$, and a second exact cancellation accounts
for many of them: a composite $(\gamma'(k-1,k)\mu,\ (k-1,k)\nu)$ with
$\mu\neq\varnothing$ and the level-$(k-2)$ composite $(\gamma'\mu',\nu)$,
where $\mu'$ is $\mu$ with its upper indices $k$ replaced by $k-1$,
have opposite weights whenever both are directed paths, by the braid
relation $t_{k-1,k}\,t_{ak}=t_{a,k-1}\,t_{k-1,k}$ applied along $\mu$
and $\kappa\kappa'=q_{k-1}$. Section~\ref{sec:block} turns this
cancellation into a bijection (Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}) and
isolates what remains for $k\geq3$ as the identity \eqref{eq:wild},
in which the directedness exceptions of the braid relation appear as
the wild chains. For $k=2$
neither phenomenon occurs, which is why the identity is immediate
there. The reduction replaces the difficulties noted after
Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation} and in Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}---that
line paths do not rotate to Bruhat paths, the backtracks, and the
diagonal case with $q$---by this one identity: left paths of distinct
composites rotate together with the chain
(Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation}), the right paths and the diagonal
are handled by Lemma~\ref{lem:right-paths} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:left-rigidity}, and the backtracks are the
content of \eqref{eq:backtrack}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The block model and the braid reduction}
\label{sec:block}

This subsection reduces the backtrack identity \eqref{eq:backtrack}
to a statement about permutations of $k$ letters, proves it in one
of the two resulting cases, and in the other case rewrites it as an
identity, \eqref{eq:wild}, that involves neither $a$ nor the
fixed value at position $k-1$ and in which no repeated label occurs.
Three facts drive this: non-fresh window values never change status
inside a chain (Lemma~\ref{lem:inert}); the factor
$1-\beta^2q_{k-1}$ is $(1-\beta E)(1+\beta E)$ for the edge
$E=(k-1,k)$, whose double traversal has weight $q_{k-1}$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:step-identity}); and the $(k-1)$-chains
containing $(k-1,k)$ are in bijection with a class of $(k-2)$-chains
by the braid relation of Lemma~\ref{lem:braid}
(Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}). Section~\ref{sec:ybe} then
recasts the identity in terms of edge operators and proves it.

\paragraph{The closed form of $A$.}
We first make Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation} explicit. For
$v\in E(u,k-1)$ let $\gamma_v$ be its $(k-1)$-chain and call its
labels with upper index $k$ its \emph{block}; the positions
$i\leq k-1$ that are lower indices of no label of $\gamma_v$ with
upper index greater than $k$ are \emph{fresh}. Say that $\gamma_v$
has a \emph{fresh suffix} if every entry of the longest strictly
decreasing suffix of its block (read by lower indices) is fresh.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:A-closed}
For every $v$,
\[
 A_{u,v}=[\,v\in E(u,k-1)\text{ and }\gamma_v\text{ has a fresh suffix}\,]\;
 \widehat C^{(k-1)}_{u,v}\;\psi_k(v),
 \qquad
 \psi_k(v)=\begin{cases} a& v(k)\neq u(k),\\ t^{-1}_{u(k)}& v(k)=u(k).\end{cases}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:distinct-rotation},
$A_{u,v}=q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(u)}\beta^{\ellength(\rho v)-\ellength(\rho u)}n_{\rho u,\rho v}\Psi(u,v)$,
and by Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}(3) a word with empty
right part survives exactly when $J=m$, that is when the whole word
is an admissible chain, and its decreasing suffix is fresh. Since
there is at most one chain from $\rho u$ to $\rho v$
(Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation}), $n_{\rho u,\rho v}\in\{0,1\}$, and it
is $1$ exactly when $v\in E(u,k-1)$ and $\gamma_v$, which has the same
labels as its rotation, has a fresh suffix. In that case the rotated
chain is a Bruhat chain, so
$\beta^{\ellength(\rho v)-\ellength(\rho u)}=\beta^{s(u,v;k-1)}$ and,
by Lemma~\ref{lem:sigma}, $q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(u)}=q^{D(u,v;k-1)}$;
finally $\Psi(u,v)=\prod_{i<k}\phi_i(u,v;k-1)\,\psi_k(v)$, because
$u(k)\in\Left(u,v;k)$ when $v(k)\neq u(k)$ (proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:left-rigidity}).
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Where the factor $1-\beta^2q_{k-1}$ comes from.}
For a formal combination $\Theta=\sum_v\theta_v\G^q_v$ with $x$-free
coefficients write
\[
 \mathcal U_k\Theta=\sum_v\theta_v\sum_{\lambda}
   \frac{(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}}{1+\beta y_{\operatorname{end}(\lambda)(k)}}
   \G^q_{\operatorname{end}(\lambda)},
 \qquad
 \mathcal R_k\Theta=\sum_v\theta_v\sum_{\varrho}\beta^{|\varrho|}q^{D(\varrho)}
   \G^q_{\operatorname{end}(\varrho)},
\]
the sums over left paths $\lambda$ and right paths $\varrho$ at $k$
from $v$, so that $\mathcal T_k=\mathcal R_k\mathcal U_k$ and
$M_{u,v}$ is the coefficient of $\G^q_v$ in
$\mathcal U_k\widehat H_{k-1}$, where
$\widehat H_{k-1}=\widehat P_{k-1}+a\beta^2q_{k-1}\widehat P_{k-2}$
and $\widehat P_j=\sum_x\widehat C^{(j)}_{u,x}\G^q_x$. Let
$\mathcal R'_{k-1}$ be defined like $\mathcal R_{k-1}$ but with the
right paths at $k-1$ restricted to upper indices $b>k$, let
$\mathcal U'_k$ be defined like $\mathcal U_k$ but with the left paths
at $k$ restricted to lower indices $a\leq k-2$, and put
$\mathcal V_k=\mathcal U'_k\mathcal R'_{k-1}$. Finally let $A^{(j)}$
denote the vector of Lemma~\ref{lem:A-closed} at level $j$, that is
$A^{(k)}=\sum_vA_{u,v}\G^q_v$ and $A^{(k-1)}$ the same with $k-1$ in
place of $k$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:step-identity}
$\mathcal U_k\mathcal T_{k-1}=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,\mathcal V_k\,\mathcal U_{k-1}$.
Consequently, if Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds at levels
$\leq k-1$ for $u$ and \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds for $(u,k-1)$, then
\eqref{eq:backtrack} holds for $(u,k)$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:step-identity}
 \mathcal V_k A^{(k-1)}=A^{(k)}+a\,\mathcal U_k\widehat P_{k-2}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A right path at $k-1$ ends with $(k-1,k)$ or not, and a left path at
$k$ begins with $(k-1,k)$ or not; the edge $(k-1,k)$ exists from every
permutation, and its two traversals have weights with product
$q_{k-1}$. Writing $E$ for the operator $\G^q_x\mapsto\kappa_x\G^q_{xt_{k-1,k}}$,
we have $\mathcal R_{k-1}=(1+\beta E)\mathcal R'_{k-1}$,
$\mathcal U_k=\mathcal U'_k(1-\beta E)$ and $E^2=q_{k-1}$, whence
$\mathcal U_k\mathcal T_{k-1}=\mathcal U'_k(1-\beta^2E^2)\mathcal R'_{k-1}\mathcal U_{k-1}$.
For the second statement, the two-step identities of
Lemma~\ref{lem:two-step} give
$\beta H_{k-1}=T_{k-1}H_{k-2}/(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})-aB^q_{k-2}$, so with
$\mathbf h_j:=\beta^jH_j\G^q_u=\widehat H_j/(1-\beta^2q_j)$ (by
\eqref{eq:step-two} and the Theorem at levels $j,j-1$) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:line-chevalley},
$\mathbf h_{k-1}=\mathcal T_{k-1}\mathbf h_{k-2}/(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})-a\widehat P_{k-2}$.
Now \eqref{eq:backtrack} for $(u,k)$ says
$\mathcal U_k\mathbf h_{k-1}=A^{(k)}$, and \eqref{eq:backtrack} for
$(u,k-1)$ says $\mathcal U_{k-1}\mathbf h_{k-2}=A^{(k-1)}$; by the
operator identity,
$\mathcal U_k\mathbf h_{k-1}=\mathcal V_k\mathcal U_{k-1}\mathbf h_{k-2}-a\,\mathcal U_k\widehat P_{k-2}
=\mathcal V_kA^{(k-1)}-a\,\mathcal U_k\widehat P_{k-2}$.
\end{proof}

Identity \eqref{eq:step-identity} contains no factor
$1-\beta^2q_{k-1}$ and no repeated edge $(k-1,k)$; all three terms are
explicit by Lemma~\ref{lem:A-closed}. We have verified it for all
$u\in S_5$ and $2\leq k\leq5$. We do not use it below, but it
explains the shape of \eqref{eq:backtrack}: the two traversals of the
junction edge that are hidden in the product
$\mathcal R_{k-1}\mathcal U_k$ account for the factor
$1-\beta^2q_{k-1}$.

\paragraph{Non-fresh values are inert.}
Fix $u$ and $k$, and an admissible $(k-1)$-chain $\eta$ from $u$ all
of whose labels have upper index greater than $k$; every left
composite at level $k-1$ or $k-2$ whose chain begins with the labels
of upper index $>k$ equal to $\eta$ will be said to have
\emph{prefix} $\eta$. Put $x_0=u\eta$ and let
$F=\{p\leq k-1:\ x_0(p)=u(p)\}$ be the set of fresh positions; by
Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation}(2) these are the positions that are lower
indices of no label of $\eta$. Position $k$ is never touched by
$\eta$. Note that level-$(k-2)$ composites with prefix $\eta$ exist
only if $\eta$ is a $(k-2)$-chain, that is if $k-1\in F$.

\begin{lemma}[Inertness]\label{lem:inert}
Let $(\gamma,\lambda)$ be a left composite at level $j\in\{k-1,k-2\}$
with prefix $\eta$, and let $i\leq j$ with $x_0(i)\neq u(i)$. Then the
status of $u(i)$ (Left or Out) is the same in $x_0$ and in the endpoint
of $\gamma$. More precisely, if $u(i)$ is at a position $p<i$ in $x_0$,
then no label of $\gamma$ after $\eta$ has lower index $p$; if it is at
a position $p>i$, $p\leq k-1$, the labels of $\gamma$ after $\eta$ move
it only to positions greater than $p$; and if it is at a position
greater than $k$, it is not moved.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The last case is clear, since the labels after $\eta$ have upper index
$k$ or $k-1$. Rotate by $\rho_\vartheta$ with $\vartheta=u(j+1)$; by
Corollary~\ref{cor:rotation}, $\gamma$ becomes a saturated chain in
the $j$-Bruhat order satisfying (P0) and (P1), with the same labels,
and statuses are determined by positions, so we may argue in the
rotated chain. Let $p_0=i,b_1,p_1,b_2,\ldots,b_r,p_r=p$ be the
successive positions of $u(i)$ along $\eta$, so that $\eta$ contains
the labels $(p_{s-1},b_s)$ and $(p_s,b_s)$ in this order, with
$b_1>b_2>\cdots>b_r>k$ by condition~3 and the distinctness of labels.
Suppose $p<i$. Then some $s$ has $p_{s-1}>p_s$; take the largest. The
prohibition lemma \cite[Lemma~2.9]{lenart2007pieri} states that a
chain satisfying (P0) and (P1) contains no subsequence
$(j',m),\ldots,(i',m),\ldots,(i',l)$ with $i'<j'\leq j<l<m$. Applied to
$(p_{s-1},b_s),(p_s,b_s)$ it shows that no later label has lower index
$p_s$ and upper index $<b_s$; every later label has upper index
$\leq b_s$, and $(p_s,b_s)$ itself is not repeated. If $s<r$ the label
$(p_s,b_{s+1})$ contradicts this, so $s=r$, and no label after
$(p_r,b_r)$, in particular none after $\eta$, has lower index $p$.
Suppose $p>i$. Position $p$ is not fresh, so $p$ is a repeated lower
index at every later label with lower index $p$, and by condition~4
the label following such a label $(p,b)$ is $(p',b)$ with $p'>p$ or
has a smaller upper index. If $(p,k)$ occurs after $\eta$, it moves
$u(i)$ to position $k$; the next label is $(p',k)$ with $p'>p$, which
moves $u(i)$ to $p'$, or has upper index $k-1$, after which position
$k$ is not touched. Position $p'$ can be touched again only by
$(p',k-1)$, which moves $u(i)$ to $k-1$; then $p'$ is repeated, so the
next label is $(p'',k-1)$ with $p''>p'$, which moves $u(i)$ to $p''$,
a position touched by no later label. If instead $(p,k-1)$ occurs, the
same argument applies from position $k-1$. In all cases $u(i)$ only
moves to positions greater than $p>i$, so it remains Out at the
endpoint of $\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The block model.}
Let $\xi$ be a sequence of $k$ distinct positive integers, regarded as
the values at positions $1,\ldots,k$, and let $F\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k-1\}$.
The quantum Bruhat graph on these $k$ positions is the one of $S_k$,
with the edge condition of Lemma~\ref{lem:arc} applied to the values
of $\xi$. A \emph{block chain at level $j\in\{k-1,k-2\}$} is a
directed path from $\xi$ with labels $(a_1,b_1),\ldots,(a_m,b_m)$,
$a_i\leq j<b_i\leq k$, that are pairwise distinct, have weakly
decreasing upper indices, and satisfy: if $i<m$ and $a_i\notin F$ or
$a_i=a_h$ for some $h<i$, then $(a_i,b_i)\prec(a_{i+1},b_{i+1})$.
Its weight is
\[
 \widehat C_F(\mu)=\beta^{m}q^{D(\mu)}\prod_{p\in F,\ p\leq j}\phi_p(\xi,x),
 \qquad
 \phi_p(\xi,x)=\begin{cases}
  t^{-1}_{\xi(p)}-a& x(p)=\xi(p),\\
  a& \xi(p)\text{ is at a position }<p\text{ in }x,\\
  t^{-1}_{\xi(p)}& \text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
\]
where $x$ is the endpoint of $\mu$; level $k-2$ is used only when
$k-1\in F$. A \emph{block composite} is a pair $(\mu,\lambda)$ of a
block chain and a left path at $k$ from its endpoint, with weight
\[
 (a\beta^2q_{k-1})^{[j=k-2]}\,(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}\,t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,\widehat C_F(\mu),
\]
$v$ the endpoint of $\lambda$; it is \emph{distinct} if it is at level
$k-1$ and no label of $\lambda$ occurs in $\mu$. Let $M^F_{\xi,v}$ and
$A^F_{\xi,v}$ be the sums of the weights of all, respectively of the
distinct, block composites with endpoint $v$. The \emph{block identity}
is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:block}
 M^F_{\xi,v}=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,A^F_{\xi,v}\qquad\text{for all }v\in S_k.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Reduction to the block model]\label{prop:block-reduction}
Let $\eta$, $x_0$ and $F$ be as above and let $\xi$ be the restriction
of $x_0$ to positions $1,\ldots,k$. The block chains for $(\xi,F)$ are
exactly the parts after $\eta$ of the admissible chains from $u$ with
prefix $\eta$, and for every $v$ the sum of the weights of the left
composites with prefix $\eta$ and endpoint $v$ equals
$c_\eta\,M^F_{\xi,v'}$, where $v'$ is the restriction of $v$ to
positions $1,\ldots,k$ and
\[
 c_\eta=\beta^{|\eta|}q^{D(\eta)}\prod_{i\leq k-1,\ i\notin F}\phi_i(u,x_0;k-1);
\]
the same holds for the distinct composites, with $A$ in place of $M$.
Consequently, if \eqref{eq:block} holds for all $(\xi,F)$, then the
backtrack identity \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds for all $u$ and $k$,
and Theorem~\ref{prop:top} follows. Conversely, every pair $(\xi,F)$
arises from some $(u,\eta)$ in this way.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The labels after $\eta$ have upper index at most $k$, so they are
distinct from those of $\eta$, and the conditions of
Definition~\ref{def:chain} for them reduce to the conditions on block
chains: a lower index $a_i$ is repeated in the whole chain exactly when
$a_i\notin F$ or $a_i$ occurs earlier after $\eta$. Directedness of a
label $(a,b)$ with $a<b\leq k$ depends only on the values at positions
$a,\ldots,b$. The weight of a left composite with prefix $\eta$ is
$\beta^{|\eta|}q^{D(\eta)}$ times the block weight, except for the
status factors $\phi_i(u,x;j)$ of the indices $i\leq j$ with
$i\notin F$, which by Lemma~\ref{lem:inert} equal $\phi_i(u,x_0;k-1)$
at every chain endpoint $x$, and which for $j=k-2$ run over the same
set of indices because $k-1\in F$; the factor $t^{-1}_{v(k)}$ is the
same on both sides, and so is distinctness. Summing over the prefixes
$\eta$ for which $u\eta$ agrees with $v$ outside positions $1,\ldots,k$
(block chains do not move the other positions) gives
$M_{u,v}=\sum_\eta c_\eta M^{F_\eta}_{\xi_\eta,v'}$ and likewise for
$A$, so \eqref{eq:block} for all $(\xi_\eta,F_\eta)$ implies
\eqref{eq:backtrack}, and Theorem~\ref{thm:backtrack-implies} applies.
For the converse, given $(\xi,F)$ with $\xi\in S_k$ after
standardization, let $u(i)=2\xi(i)-1$ for $i\leq k$, enumerate the
positions $p\notin F$ as $p_1,\ldots,p_r$, put $u(k+r+1-s)=2\xi(p_s)$,
and fill the remaining positions with the remaining values. Then
$\eta=(p_1,k+r),(p_2,k+r-1),\ldots,(p_r,k+1)$ is a chain of Bruhat
covers (each label exchanges the adjacent values $2\xi(p_s)-1$ and
$2\xi(p_s)$) with distinct lower indices, hence admissible, and
$u\eta$ restricted to positions $\leq k$ has the relative order of
$\xi$, with fresh set $F$.
\end{proof}

We have verified \eqref{eq:block} for every $\xi\in S_k$ and every
$F$ for $k\leq5$, and for a random sample of configurations with
$k=6$; by Proposition~\ref{prop:block-reduction} this covers all
$u\in S_\infty$ for $k\leq5$.

\begin{proposition}[The block identity when position $k-1$ is not fresh]
\label{prop:block-notfresh}
If $k-1\notin F$, then \eqref{eq:block} holds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
There are no level-$(k-2)$ composites. If $(k-1,k)$ occurs in a block
chain it is the last label, since $k-1$ is a repeated lower index and
no label $(a,k)$ with $a>k-1$ exists. By Lemma~\ref{lem:peel}(1),
$\mu\mapsto\mu(k-1,k)$ is a bijection from the block chains not
containing $(k-1,k)$ onto those containing it, and
$\widehat C_F(\mu(k-1,k))=\beta\kappa\widehat C_F(\mu)$ with
$\kappa$ the weight of the edge $(k-1,k)$ from the endpoint of $\mu$:
no fresh value sits at position $k-1$, and a fresh value at position
$k$ moves to $k-1$, where it is still Out. Write $x$ for the endpoint
of $\mu$ and $x'=xt_{k-1,k}$, with $\kappa'$ the weight of $x'\to x$,
$\kappa\kappa'=q_{k-1}$. The block composites are of four kinds:
(i) $(\mu,\lambda)$ with $(k-1,k)\notin\mu\lambda$; (ii)
$(\mu,(k-1,k)\lambda')$ with $(k-1,k)\notin\mu$; (iii)
$(\mu(k-1,k),\lambda')$ with $\lambda'$ a left path from $x'$ avoiding
$k-1$; (iv) $(\mu(k-1,k),(k-1,k)\lambda)$. In (ii) and (iii), $\lambda'$
is a left path from $x'$ with lower indices $\leq k-2$, and the two
weights are $-\beta\kappa$ and $\beta\kappa$ times the same quantity,
so (ii) and (iii) cancel in pairs, also after restricting to distinct
composites. In (iv), $\lambda$ is a left path from $x$, and the weight
is $\beta\kappa\cdot(-\beta)\kappa'=-\beta^2q_{k-1}$ times the weight
of the composite $(\mu,\lambda)$ of kind (i). The composites of kind
(i) are distinct by Lemma~\ref{lem:repeat}, those of kind (iv) are
not, so $M^F_{\xi,v}=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\sum_{\text{(i)}}$ and
$A^F_{\xi,v}=\sum_{\text{(i)}}$.
\end{proof}

In the language of Proposition~\ref{prop:block-reduction}, the
backtrack identity therefore holds prefix by prefix whenever the
prefix $\eta$ moves position $k-1$. From now on $k-1\in F$, and we
write $t^\ast=t_{\xi(k-1)}$.

\paragraph{The braid bijection.}
A block chain at level $k-2$ is $\mu\nu$ with $\mu$ the labels of
upper index $k$ and $\nu$ those of upper index $k-1$. Let
$\rho=\rho_{\vartheta}$ with $\vartheta=\xi(k)$. Call $\mu\nu$
\emph{tame} if no lower index of $\nu$ occurs in $\mu$ and every edge of
$\nu$ is a Bruhat edge after rotation by $\rho$ (in particular if
$\nu=\varnothing$), and \emph{wild} otherwise. For a sequence $\nu$ of
labels of upper index $k-1$ let $\tilde\nu$ be the same sequence with
upper index $k$.

\begin{lemma}[Braid bijection]\label{lem:braid-bijection}
Let $k-1\in F$. The map $\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu\mapsto\mu\nu$ is a
bijection from the block chains at level $k-1$ containing $(k-1,k)$
onto the tame block chains at level $k-2$. If $\gamma=\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$
corresponds to $c=\mu\nu$ with endpoint $x$, then $\gamma$ ends at
$xt_{k-1,k}$, every edge of $\gamma$ is Bruhat after rotation by
$\rho$, and
\[
 \widehat C_F(\gamma)=\beta\kappa_x\,\widehat C_F(c)\cdot
 \begin{cases} a&\nu\neq\varnothing,\\ t^{\ast}&\nu=\varnothing,\end{cases}
\]
where $\kappa_x$ is the weight of the edge $x\to xt_{k-1,k}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A level-$(k-1)$ block chain containing $(k-1,k)$ has the form
$\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$ with all lower indices of $\mu$ and $\tilde\nu$ at
most $k-2$, since labels are distinct. Admissibility of
$\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$ and of $\mu\nu$ are equivalent: labels are
distinct in both; upper indices are weakly decreasing in both; the
label after the last label $(a,k)$ of $\mu$ is $\succ(a,k)$ in both
(it is $(k-1,k)$, respectively a label of upper index $k-1$); at
$(k-1,k)$ no condition is imposed because $k-1\in F$; and inside
$\tilde\nu$ and $\nu$ the repeated lower indices and the required
inequalities between consecutive lower indices are the same. The two
label sequences have the same product of transpositions, by the braid
relation $t_{k-1,k}t_{bk}=t_{b,k-1}t_{k-1,k}$ applied to each label of
$\nu$ in turn.

Directedness. Let $x_\mu$ be the endpoint of $\mu$ and $c^\ast=\xi(k-1)$,
$c_\mu=x_\mu(k)$. The path $\mu(k-1,k)$ is directed, has distinct
labels of upper index $k$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} (with $k-1$
in place of $k$) its edges are Bruhat after rotation; hence
$c^\ast<c_\mu$ in the rotated order. Suppose $\gamma$ is directed; then
all its edges are rotated-Bruhat, again by Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation}. Along $\tilde\nu$ the value at
position $k$ decreases from $c^\ast$, and the cover condition for a
label $(b,k)$ involves the positions $b+1,\ldots,k-1$, where position
$k-1$ holds $c_\mu$, which exceeds every value that position $k$ takes
along $\tilde\nu$. Therefore the same label $(b,k-1)$, applied in
$\mu\nu$ at the corresponding moment, exchanges the same two values,
with the same intermediate values except that $c_\mu$ is no longer
among them; it is a rotated-Bruhat edge, and $\mu\nu$ is tame.
Conversely, if $\mu\nu$ is tame the same comparison shows that each
label $(b,k)$ of $\tilde\nu$ is a Bruhat cover after rotation, the only
additional intermediate value being $c_\mu$, which lies above the
interval in question. By Corollary~\ref{cor:cyclic} the two paths are
directed in the quantum Bruhat graph, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:braid},
applied at each step, they have the same quantum weight up to the
weight $\kappa_x$ of the final edge $x\to xt_{k-1,k}$ of the braided
form $\mu\nu(k-1,k)$; the endpoints agree.

Weights. The chain $\gamma$ has one more label than $c$, which gives
the factor $\beta$, and $q^{D(\gamma)}=\kappa_xq^{D(c)}$ was just
shown. For $p\in F$, $p\leq k-2$, the value $\xi(p)$ occupies the same
position in the endpoints of $\gamma$ and of $c$ unless it is at
position $k-1$ or $k$, where it is Out in both. The value $c^\ast$ is
moved by $(k-1,k)$ to position $k$ and, if $\tilde\nu\neq\varnothing$,
by its first label $(b_1,k)$ to position $b_1<k-1$, where it stays;
so $\phi_{k-1}$ is $a$ if $\nu\neq\varnothing$ and $t^\ast$ otherwise.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The reduction.}
For a block chain $c$ at level $k-2$ and a left path $\lambda$ at $k$
from its endpoint put
\[
 \varpi(c,\lambda)=(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}\,t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,\widehat C_F(c),
\]
the weight of the level-$(k-2)$ composite without its prefactor
$a\beta^2q_{k-1}$. Let
\begin{align*}
 X_v&=\sum_{c\text{ wild}}\ \sum_{\lambda}\varpi(c,\lambda),\\
 Y_v&=\sum_{c\text{ tame}}\ \sum_{\substack{\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'\\
        \lambda'\text{ shares a lower index with }c}}\varpi(c,\lambda),
\end{align*}
both over composites with endpoint $v$.

\begin{theorem}[Braid reduction]\label{thm:braid-reduction}
Let $k-1\in F$. For every $v\in S_k$,
\[
 M^F_{\xi,v}-(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})A^F_{\xi,v}
 =a\bigl(\beta^2q_{k-1}X_v-(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})Y_v\bigr).
\]
Hence the block identity \eqref{eq:block} holds for $(\xi,F)$ if and
only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wild}
 \beta^2q_{k-1}\,X_v=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\,Y_v\qquad\text{for all }v\in S_k.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
