We list all block composites with endpoint $v$ according to the
level-$(k-2)$ chain $c=\mu\nu$ they are built from. Write $x$ for the
endpoint of $c$, $x'=xt_{k-1,k}$, $\kappa=\kappa_x$, $\kappa'$ for the
weight of $x'\to x$, so $\kappa\kappa'=q_{k-1}$, and note that a left
path from $x'$ beginning with $(k-1,k)$ is $(k-1,k)\lambda$ with
$\lambda$ a left path from $x$ of lower indices $\leq k-2$, and
conversely.

(a) Level $k-2$: the composite $(c,\lambda)$, of weight
$a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi(c,\lambda)$, for every $c$ and $\lambda$.

(b) Level $k-1$, chain without $(k-1,k)$: such a chain is a level-$(k-2)$
chain $c$ with $\nu=\varnothing$, and $\widehat C_F(c)$ at level $k-1$
equals $(t^\ast-a)\widehat C_F(c)$ at level $k-2$. The composite
$(c,\lambda)$ has weight $(t^\ast-a)\varpi(c,\lambda)$ for every left
path $\lambda$ from $x$.

(c) Level $k-1$, chain $\gamma=\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$ containing
$(k-1,k)$: by Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}, $\gamma$ corresponds to
a tame $c$ and ends at $x'$. If the left path avoids $k-1$, it is a
left path $\lambda$ from $x'$ with lower indices $\leq k-2$, and the
weight is
$\beta\kappa\,\theta\,\widehat C_F(c)(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}t^{-1}_{v(k)}
=-\theta\,\varpi(c,(k-1,k)\lambda)$, where $\theta=a$ if
$\nu\neq\varnothing$ and $\theta=t^\ast$ otherwise. If the left path is
$(k-1,k)\lambda$, then $\lambda$ is a left path from $x$ and the weight
is $\beta\kappa\theta\widehat C_F(c)\cdot(-\beta)\kappa'\cdot(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}q^{D(\lambda)}t^{-1}_{v(k)}
=-\beta^2q_{k-1}\theta\,\varpi(c,\lambda)$.

Now fix $c$ and a left path $\lambda$ from $x$ and collect the terms.
If $c$ is wild, only (a) contributes: $a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi(c,\lambda)$.
If $c$ is tame with $\nu\neq\varnothing$ and $\lambda$ avoids $k-1$,
(a) and the second part of (c) give
$a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi-a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi=0$; if $\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$,
(a) and the first part of (c) give
$a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi-a\varpi=-a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\varpi(c,\lambda)$.
If $\nu=\varnothing$ and $\lambda$ avoids $k-1$, (a), (b) and the second
part of (c) give
$(a\beta^2q_{k-1}+(t^\ast-a)-\beta^2q_{k-1}t^\ast)\varpi
=(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})(t^\ast-a)\varpi(c,\lambda)$; if
$\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$, they give
$(a\beta^2q_{k-1}+(t^\ast-a)-t^\ast)\varpi=-a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\varpi(c,\lambda)$.

The distinct composites are: those of (b) whose left path avoids
$k-1$, all of which are distinct by Lemma~\ref{lem:repeat}; those of
(b) with $\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$ such that $\lambda'$ shares no
lower index with $c$; and those of the first part of (c) whose
$\lambda$ shares no lower index with $\mu$ or $\nu$. The second part
of (c) repeats $(k-1,k)$. Hence $(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})A^F_{\xi,v}$ is, for
$\nu=\varnothing$ and $\lambda$ avoiding $k-1$,
$(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})(t^\ast-a)\varpi$; for $\nu=\varnothing$ and
$\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$ with $\lambda'$ sharing no index with $c$,
$(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})(t^\ast-a-t^\ast)\varpi=-a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\varpi$;
for $c$ tame with $\nu\ne\varnothing$ and $\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$
sharing no index, $-a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\varpi$; and $0$ otherwise.
Subtracting, every term cancels except
$-a(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\varpi(c,\lambda)$ for tame $c$ and
$\lambda=(k-1,k)\lambda'$ with $\lambda'$ sharing a lower index with
$c$, and $a\beta^2q_{k-1}\varpi(c,\lambda)$ for wild $c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The reduced identity]\label{rem:wild}
Identity \eqref{eq:wild} is the remaining content of the backtrack
identity, hence of Theorem~\ref{prop:top}, for $k\geq3$. Its left side
runs over level-$(k-2)$ chains that are wild, that is whose
$(k-1)$-labels either reuse a lower index of $\mu$ or contain an edge
that is quantum after rotation by $\xi(k)$, followed by arbitrary left
paths; its right side runs over tame chains followed by $(k-1,k)$ and
a left path that returns to a lower index of the chain. Neither $a$
nor $t^\ast$ appears. For $k=2$ there are no labels of upper index
$k-1$ and no wild chains, both sides vanish, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:backtrack-known}(i) is recovered. We have
verified \eqref{eq:wild} for all $\xi\in S_k$ and all $F\ni k-1$ with
$k\leq5$. The same computations show that $X_v$ and $Y_v$ are each
rigid at $v$ in the sense of Section~\ref{sec:backtrack}---each is a
$y$-free multiple of $\Psi(\xi,v)$---although neither is rigid word by
word; the rigidity is produced by cancellations between words related
by the braid relation $(k-1,k)(a,k)=(a,k-1)(k-1,k)$, the three forms
$(a,k)(a,k-1)=(a,k-1)(k-1,k)=(k-1,k)(a,k)$ of a $3$-cycle, and the
commutation $(a,k)(b,k-1)=(b,k-1)(a,k)$ for $a\neq b$. For instance,
for $\xi=213$, $F=\{1,2\}$, $k=3$ and $v=123$, the wild chains are
$(1,2)$ and $(1,3)(1,2)$, with
$X_v=\beta q_1(1-\beta^2q_2)t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1}$ coming from the two
composites $((1,2),\varnothing)$ and $((1,3)(1,2),(2,3))$, the second
being the first with a backtrack inserted through the $3$-cycle, and
$Y_v=\beta^3q_1q_2t_2^{-1}t_3^{-1}$ comes from the single composite
$((1,3),(2,3)(1,3))$. Identity \eqref{eq:wild} is proved in
Section~\ref{sec:ybe} (Theorem~\ref{thm:block}), which completes the
proof of Theorem~\ref{prop:top}.

The same computations ($k\leq5$) exhibit a finer structure. Both
sides of \eqref{eq:wild} are polynomials in $q$, so \eqref{eq:wild}
splits according to the $q$-degree $D$ of the word $c\lambda$ (with
the convention that the factor $\beta^2q_{k-1}$ is counted in $D$):
writing $X_{v,D}$, $Y_{v,D}$ for the parts of degree $D$, it reads
$Y_{v,D}=\beta^2(X_{v,D-e_{k-1}}+Y_{v,D-e_{k-1}})$. Numerically,
$X_{v,D}$ and $Y_{v,D}$ are each $y$-free multiples of $\Psi(\xi,v)$,
and for every $v$ there are a sign $\epsilon_v\in\{0,\pm1\}$ and a
degree $D_v$ such that
$X_v=\epsilon_v(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})\beta^{m}q^{D_v}\Psi(\xi,v)/a$ and
$Y_v=\epsilon_v\beta^2q_{k-1}\beta^{m}q^{D_v}\Psi(\xi,v)/a$, with
$m=\ellength(v)-\ellength(\xi)+2|D_v|$; that is, $X_v+Y_v$ is a single
monomial in $q$. When $v(k)=\xi(k)$ the monomial is the weight of the
unique level-$(k-2)$ chain to $v$ (and $\epsilon_v=1$ exactly when
that chain is wild); when $v(k)\neq\xi(k)$ both signs occur.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Operator form, Yang--Baxter relations, and the proof of the
block identity}
\label{sec:ybe}

In this subsection we rewrite the block identity as an identity
between operators built from the edges of the quantum Bruhat graph.
The edge operators satisfy the Yang--Baxter relations of
Brenti, Fomin and Postnikov \cite{brenti1999mixed}, which make the
braid move of Lemma~\ref{lem:braid} and the $3$-cycle relations of
Remark~\ref{rem:wild} exact; the wild chains are identified by a
single inequality; the left paths are eliminated altogether, leaving
an identity between chains (Proposition~\ref{prop:chain-form}). When
no position below $k-1$ is fresh the block identity is then proved
(Theorem~\ref{thm:F-minimal}) by an explicit bijection between two
families of wild chains described through right-to-left maxima. For
general $F$ the chain identity splits into components indexed by the
subsets of the fresh positions below $k-1$, of which the top one is
the case just proved; the other components involve chains with
descents, whose structure is worked out in
Lemmas~\ref{lem:shape}--\ref{lem:typeB}, and the same bijection,
extended to these chains, proves them all (Theorem~\ref{thm:block}).
This completes the proof of the backtrack identity, hence of
Theorem~\ref{prop:top} (Corollary~\ref{cor:main}).

\paragraph{Conventions.}
Throughout, $k\geq3$, $\xi$ and $F\ni k-1$ are as in the block model,
and $F'=F\cap\{1,\ldots,k-2\}$. Rotating all values by
$\rho_{\xi(k)}$ (Section~\ref{sec:rotation}) changes the quantum
weight of every directed path from $\xi$ to $v$ in the block by the
same factor $q^{\sigma(v)-\sigma(\xi)}$, $\sigma_j(x)=\#\{i\leq j:x(i)\leq\xi(k)\}$,
because an edge $(a,b)$ with $a\leq j<b$ changes $\sigma_j$ by $+1$
exactly when it is quantum before and Bruhat after rotation, by $-1$
in the opposite case, and otherwise not at all
(Corollary~\ref{cor:cyclic}); the status factors depend only on
positions, and tameness is defined through the rotated graph. Hence
both sides of \eqref{eq:block} at $v$ are multiplied by the same unit,
and we may and do assume that $\xi(k)=k$ is the largest value (the
\emph{clean frame}). Then by Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} every directed
path from $\xi$ with pairwise distinct labels of upper index $k$ is a
chain of Bruhat covers. We write $c^\ast=\xi(k-1)$ and
$t^\ast=t_{c^\ast}^{-1}$ as before.

Let $V$ be the free module on the symbols $\G_x$, $x\in S_k$, over the
coefficient ring. For a label $(a,b)$, $a<b\leq k$, let $E_{ab}$ be
the operator with $E_{ab}\G_x=\kappa\,\G_{xt_{ab}}$ if $x\to xt_{ab}$
is an edge of the quantum Bruhat graph of weight $\kappa$, and
$E_{ab}\G_x=0$ otherwise. For a word $w=\ell_1\ell_2\cdots\ell_r$ of
labels put $P[w]=E_{\ell_r}\cdots E_{\ell_1}$, so that
$P[w]\G_x$ is the weight of $w$ times $\G_{\operatorname{end}}$ if $w$
is a directed path from $x$ and $0$ otherwise; products of operators
are composed from right to left. We abbreviate
\[
 E=E_{k-1,k},\qquad K_a=E_{ak},\qquad L_a=E_{a,k-1}\qquad(1\leq a\leq k-2).
\]

\begin{lemma}[Yang--Baxter relations]\label{lem:ybe}
\begin{enumerate}
\item $E_{ab}E_{cd}=E_{cd}E_{ab}$ whenever $\{a,b\}\cap\{c,d\}=\varnothing$.
\item For $a<b<c$ and a formal variable $\varepsilon$,
\[
 (1+\varepsilon E_{ab})(1+\varepsilon E_{ac})(1+\varepsilon E_{bc})
 =(1+\varepsilon E_{bc})(1+\varepsilon E_{ac})(1+\varepsilon E_{ab}).
\]
Explicitly, in degree two,
\begin{align}
 P[(a,b)(b,c)]&=P[(b,c)(a,c)]+P[(a,c)(a,b)],\label{eq:ybe-C}\\
 P[(b,c)(a,b)]&=P[(a,b)(a,c)]+P[(a,c)(b,c)],\label{eq:ybe-Cinv}
\end{align}
and in degree three $P[(a,b)(a,c)(b,c)]=P[(b,c)(a,c)(a,b)]$.
\item $E_{a,a+1}^2=q_a$, and $E_{ab}^2=0$ if $b-a\geq2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) and (2) are Corollary~4.1 of \cite{brenti1999mixed}, equations
(2.3)--(2.4) there, for the quantum Bruhat operators $Q_\tau$ with the
multiplicative function $E(\epsilon_a-\epsilon_b)=q_a\cdots q_{b-1}$;
the operators of \cite{brenti1999mixed} act by left multiplication
$w\mapsto\tau w$, and inversion $w\mapsto w^{-1}$, which preserves
lengths, identifies them with our edge operators, which act by right
multiplication. In (2) the two sides agree in degrees $0$ and $1$, and
in degree two each side is a sum of three terms; the group elements
$t_{ab}t_{bc}=t_{bc}t_{ac}=t_{ac}t_{ab}$ and
$t_{bc}t_{ab}=t_{ab}t_{ac}=t_{ac}t_{bc}$ are the two $3$-cycles, and
collecting the terms according to the $3$-cycle they represent gives
\eqref{eq:ybe-C} and \eqref{eq:ybe-Cinv}. (3) The adjacent
transposition is an edge from every permutation, and its two
traversals have weights with product $q_a$. For $b-a\geq2$, a Bruhat
edge $(a,b)$ requires the values at the positions strictly between $a$
and $b$ to lie outside the interval spanned by the two exchanged
values, while the reverse quantum edge would require them to lie
inside it. We have also checked (1)--(3) directly for $k\leq6$.
\end{proof}

Since each $P[w]\G_x$ is either $0$ or a single monomial times a
single basis vector, \eqref{eq:ybe-C} with $(a,b,c)=(a,k-1,k)$ says:
for every $x$, the path $(a,k-1)(k-1,k)$ is directed from $x$ if and
only if exactly one of $(k-1,k)(a,k)$ and $(a,k)(a,k-1)$ is, and then
the weights agree. This sharpens Lemma~\ref{lem:braid}: the braid
move fails exactly when the wild pattern $(a,k)(a,k-1)$ is directed.

\begin{lemma}[Wild chains]\label{lem:wild-char}
Let $c=\mu\nu$ be a level-$(k-2)$ block chain with endpoint $x$. Then
$c$ is wild if and only if $x(k-1)$ lies in
$\operatorname{arc}(\xi(k-1),\xi(k))\cup\{\xi(k)\}$; in the clean
frame, if and only if $x(k-1)>c^\ast$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Work in the clean frame. Rotate by $\rho_{c^\ast}$; by
Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} with $k-2$ in place of $k$ (so
$\vartheta=\xi(k-1)=c^\ast$), every edge of $c$ is Bruhat after this
rotation. A Bruhat edge $(c,k-1)$ replaces the value at $k-1$ by the
smaller value at $c$, and no other label of $c$ touches position
$k-1$; so the value at position $k-1$, which starts at the rotated
maximum $c^\ast$, strictly decreases in the rotated order
$c^\ast+1<\cdots<k<1<\cdots<c^\ast$ at each label of $\nu$. Thus it runs first through values $\leq c^\ast$ and, once it
has become larger than $c^\ast$, stays larger. By
Corollary~\ref{cor:cyclic} an edge $(c,k-1)$ of $\nu$ applied at $y$
is quantum in the clean frame exactly when $y(k-1)\leq c^\ast<y(c)$,
that is, exactly when it carries the value at $k-1$ from $\leq c^\ast$
to $>c^\ast$. Hence $\nu$ has a clean-frame quantum edge if and only
if $x(k-1)>c^\ast$. Finally, if $\nu$ reuses a lower index $a_i$ of
$\mu$, the value entering position $k-1$ at that label is the value
$e_{i-1}$ that position $k$ held before the label $(a_i,k)$ of $\mu$,
and $e_{i-1}>c^\ast$: along $\mu$ the value at $k$ decreases from
$e_0=k$ through $e_1>\cdots>e_m$, position $k-1$ lies strictly between
every $a_i$ and $k$ and holds $c^\ast$, so the cover condition gives
$c^\ast\notin(e_i,e_{i-1})$ for all $i$, whence $c^\ast<e_m$. So in
this case too $x(k-1)>c^\ast$, and conversely $x(k-1)>c^\ast$ forces a
quantum edge of $\nu$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The block identity as an operator identity.}
Put
\[
 \mathcal U'=(1-\beta K_1)(1-\beta K_2)\cdots(1-\beta K_{k-2}),\qquad
 \mathcal K=(1+\beta K_{k-2})\cdots(1+\beta K_1),
\]
so that $\mathcal U'$ is the sum over left paths at $k$ with lower
indices $\leq k-2$ of $(-\beta)^{|\lambda|}P[\lambda]$, the operator
$\mathcal U_k$ of Section~\ref{sec:block} is $\mathcal U'(1-\beta E)$,
and $\mathcal K$ is the sum over strictly increasing sequences
$a_1<\cdots<a_m\leq k-2$ of $\beta^mP[(a_1,k)\cdots(a_m,k)]$, the
Left-free chains with indices below $k-1$. Let $\mathcal U'_0$ be
defined like $\mathcal U'$ with each $K_a$ replaced by its Bruhat
part. By Lemma~\ref{lem:ybe}(3), $K_a^2=0$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:UK}
 \mathcal U'\mathcal K=\mathcal K\mathcal U'=1,
\end{equation}
the factors cancelling from the middle outwards. Let $\Theta$ be the
diagonal operator $\G_v\mapsto t^{-1}_{v(k)}\G_v$, let $\Pi_w$ and
$\Pi_t=1-\Pi_w$ be the projections onto the span of the $\G_x$ with
$x(k-1)>c^\ast$, respectively $x(k-1)\leq c^\ast$, and put
\[
 \mathcal P=\sum_{c}\widehat C_F(c)\,\G_{\operatorname{end}(c)},\qquad
 \mathcal Q_\varnothing=\sum_{c:\ \nu=\varnothing}\widehat C_F(c)\,\G_{\operatorname{end}(c)},
\]
the sums over the level-$(k-2)$ block chains $c=\mu\nu$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:wild-char}, $\Pi_w\mathcal P$ and $\Pi_t\mathcal P$
are the wild and the tame parts of $\mathcal P$.

\begin{proposition}[Operator form]\label{prop:operator-form}
With $B=\beta^2q_{k-1}$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_vM^F_{\xi,v}\G_v&=\Theta\,\mathcal U'\Bigl[(1-B)(t^\ast-a)\mathcal Q_\varnothing
  +a(1-B)\beta E\,\Pi_t\mathcal P+aB(1-\beta E)\Pi_w\mathcal P\Bigr],
  \label{eq:M-operator}\\
 \sum_vA^F_{\xi,v}\G_v&=\Theta\Bigl[\mathcal U'(t^\ast-a)\mathcal Q_\varnothing
  +a\,\mathcal U'_0\,\beta E\,\Pi_t\mathcal P\Bigr],\label{eq:A-operator}
\end{align}
and \eqref{eq:wild} is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:W}
 \Omega\,\mathcal P=0,\qquad
 \Omega:=(1-B)(\mathcal U'-\mathcal U'_0)\,\beta E\,\Pi_t+B\,\mathcal U'(1-\beta E)\,\Pi_w .
\end{equation}
Moreover, writing $\mathcal P^0=\sum_c\beta^{|c|}q^{D(c)}\G_{\operatorname{end}(c)}$
for the chain sum without status factors, \eqref{eq:W} holds if and
only if $\Omega\,\Pi_S\mathcal P^0=0$ for every $S\subseteq F'$, where
$\Pi_S$ projects onto the span of the $\G_x$ in which no value
$\xi(p)$, $p\in S$, is Left and no value $\xi(p)$, $p\in F'\setminus S$,
is fixed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Level-$(k-1)$ block chains without $(k-1,k)$ are the chains
$c=\mu$ with $\nu=\varnothing$, with weight $(t^\ast-a)\widehat C_F(c)$,
and those containing $(k-1,k)$ correspond by
Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection} to the tame chains $c$, with weight
$\beta\kappa\theta_c\widehat C_F(c)$ at the endpoint
$\operatorname{end}(c)t_{k-1,k}$, that is, their sum is
$\beta E\bigl(t^\ast\mathcal Q_\varnothing+a(\Pi_t\mathcal P-\mathcal Q_\varnothing)\bigr)$.
Hence the sum $\widehat H^F$ of the weights of the level-$(k-1)$
block chains plus $aB$ times the sum of the weights of the
level-$(k-2)$ block chains, each placed at its endpoint, is
$\widehat H^F=(t^\ast-a)\mathcal Q_\varnothing+\beta E(t^\ast\mathcal Q_\varnothing+a\Pi_t\mathcal P-a\mathcal Q_\varnothing)+aB\mathcal P$,
and by definition of the block composites
$\sum_vM^F_{\xi,v}\G_v=\Theta\,\mathcal U'(1-\beta E)\widehat H^F$.
Expanding with $(1-\beta E)\beta E=\beta E-B$ gives
\eqref{eq:M-operator}. For \eqref{eq:A-operator}, the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:braid-reduction} lists the distinct composites: those
of kind (b) with left path avoiding $k-1$, with total
$\Theta\mathcal U'(t^\ast-a)\mathcal Q_\varnothing$; those of kind (b)
with left path $(k-1,k)\lambda'$ and those of kind (c) with left path
$\lambda'$ avoiding $k-1$, in both cases with $\lambda'$ sharing no
lower index with $c$, whose weights add up to
$a\,(-\beta)^{|\lambda'|}q^{D}t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,\beta\kappa\,\widehat C_F(c)$
for each tame $c$ and each such $\lambda'$, by the computation in that
proof. The word $\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu\lambda'$ has pairwise distinct
labels of upper index $k$ exactly when $\lambda'$ shares no lower index
with $c$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation} all its edges are then Bruhat,
and conversely a repeated label is a quantum edge, since the position
it touches holds a value exceeding the value at $k$ (as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:repeat}). Therefore the left paths $\lambda'$ sharing no
index with $c$ are exactly the left paths from
$\operatorname{end}(c)t_{k-1,k}$ consisting of Bruhat edges, and the
sum is $\Theta\,a\,\mathcal U'_0\beta E\Pi_t\mathcal P$. Subtracting
$(1-B)$ times \eqref{eq:A-operator} from \eqref{eq:M-operator} and
dividing by $a$ gives $\Theta\Omega\mathcal P$, which by
Theorem~\ref{thm:braid-reduction} is $\sum_v(\beta^2q_{k-1}X_v-(1-\beta^2q_{k-1})Y_v)\G_v$;
as $\Theta$ is invertible, \eqref{eq:wild} is equivalent to \eqref{eq:W}.

For the last assertion write $s_p=t^{-1}_p-a$ for $p\in F'$. The
status factor of $\xi(p)$ at a chain endpoint $x$ is $s_p$, $a$ or
$s_p+a$ according as $\xi(p)$ is fixed, Left or Out in $x$, that is,
$s_p(1-[\xi(p)\text{ Left}])+a(1-[\xi(p)\text{ fixed}])$. Expanding
the product over $p\in F'$,
$\mathcal P=\sum_{S\subseteq F'}s^S a^{|F'\setminus S|}\,\Pi_S\mathcal P^0$,
where $s^S=\prod_{p\in S}s_p$. The operator $\Omega$ involves neither
$a$ nor the equivariant parameters, and the monomials $s^S$ are
linearly independent over $\mathbb Z[\beta,q,a]$; hence
$\Omega\mathcal P=0$ if and only if $\Omega\Pi_S\mathcal P^0=0$ for
every $S$.
\end{proof}

The last assertion says that \eqref{eq:wild} is equivalent to a family
of identities that involve neither $a$ nor $y$: for each
$S\subseteq F'$, the signed sum of the wild composites and the
composites counted by $Y_v$ whose chain endpoint has no Left value
among $\{\xi(p):p\in S\}$ and no fixed value among
$\{\xi(p):p\in F'\setminus S\}$ vanishes. For $S=F'$ these are the
composites whose chain has no Left value below $k-1$ at its endpoint;
for $F'=\varnothing$ there is a single identity, treated next.

\begin{remark}[The case $a=0$ in one line]
At $a=0$ only Left-free chains contribute to $\widehat P_{k-1}$, each
with status factor $\prod_{p\in F}t^{-1}_{p}$, and the level-$(k-2)$
part of $\widehat H^F$ vanishes. The Left-free level-$(k-1)$ chains are
the increasing sequences in $\{1,\ldots,k-1\}$, with operator
$(1+\beta E)\mathcal K$, so by \eqref{eq:UK}
\[
 \mathcal U_k(1+\beta E)\mathcal K
 =\mathcal U'(1-\beta E)(1+\beta E)\mathcal K
 =(1-B)\,\mathcal U'\mathcal K=1-B,
\]
which is the block identity at $a=0$: $M^F|_{a=0}=(1-B)A^F|_{a=0}$,
both sides being supported at $v=\xi$. This is the block-model form of
Proposition~\ref{prop:backtrack-known}(ii).
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Eliminating the left paths.}
The operator $\mathcal U'$ can be removed from the block identity
altogether, at the price of a closed form for $A^F$. Let $c=\mu\nu$ be
a level-$(k-2)$ block chain with $\nu=(c_1,k-1)\cdots(c_n,k-1)$,
$n\geq1$, and $c_n\in F'$. The value $\xi(c_n)$ sits at position $k-1$
in $\operatorname{end}(c)$, where it is Out, so $\widehat C_F(c)$
contains the factor $t^{-1}_{\xi(c_n)}$; let $\widehat C^\flat_F(c)$
denote $\widehat C_F(c)$ with this factor replaced by $a$, and put
\[
 \mathcal P^\flat_t=\sum_{\substack{c=\mu\nu\text{ tame}\\ \nu\neq\varnothing,\ c_n\in F'}}
   \widehat C^\flat_F(c)\,\G_{\operatorname{end}(c)},
 \qquad
 \mathcal Q=\mathcal Q_\varnothing+\mathcal P^\flat_t .
\]
For $F=\{k-1\}$ we have $F'=\varnothing$ and $\mathcal Q=\mathcal Q_\varnothing$.

\begin{proposition}[Chain form of the block identity]\label{prop:chain-form}
Let $k-1\in F$. The block identity \eqref{eq:block} for $(\xi,F)$ is
equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-form}
 E\,\Pi_t\mathcal P+\beta q_{k-1}\,\mathcal P=(1+\beta E)\,\mathcal K E\,\mathcal Q .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first compute $A^F$. The distinct block composites are those whose
word has pairwise distinct labels, and the distinct composites with a
given word $\omega$ are the valid cuts of $\omega$ in the sense of
Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-word}. That proposition and its proof
concern the composites with one fixed word and apply verbatim in the
block model, where the fresh positions are those in $F$ and the
non-fresh positions carry no status factor; in the clean frame every
edge of a word with distinct labels is Bruhat (Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation}),
so no power of $q$ occurs. Hence, exactly as in Lemma~\ref{lem:A-closed},
$A^F_{\xi,v}$ is the sum of $\Sigma(\gamma)$ over the level-$(k-1)$
block chains $\gamma$ with endpoint $v$ and a fresh suffix, where
\[
 \Sigma(\gamma)=\beta^{|\gamma|}\,t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,a^{S}
 \prod_{p\in F\text{ not in }\gamma}(t^{-1}_{\xi(p)}-a)\prod_{r\leq|\gamma|-S}\psi_r ,
\]
$S$ is the length of the longest strictly decreasing suffix of the
sequence of lower indices of $\gamma$, and $\psi_r$ is $a$ if the $r$th
lower index is fresh and exceeds the next one, $t^{-1}_{\xi(a)}$ if it
is a fresh index $a$ that is smaller than the next one or is the last
index before the suffix, and $1$ if it is not fresh.

The words of the composites counted by $\Theta(t^\ast-a)\mathcal U'\mathcal Q_\varnothing$
are the words $\mu\lambda$ without the label $(k-1,k)$; these
composites are all distinct (Lemma~\ref{lem:repeat}), and grouping them
by their words shows that $\Theta(t^\ast-a)\mathcal U'\mathcal Q_\varnothing$
is the part of $A^F$ coming from the chains $\gamma$ without
$(k-1,k)$. The remaining chains are $\gamma=\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$ with
$c=\mu\nu$ tame (Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}). The lower indices
of $\gamma$ are $a_1,\ldots,a_m,k-1,c_1,\ldots,c_n$; if $n=0$ the
decreasing suffix is $(k-1)$, and if $n\geq1$ it ends with $c_n$ and
its other entries are descents of an admissible chain, hence fresh. So
$\gamma$ has a fresh suffix if and only if $n=0$ or $c_n\in F'$. For
such $\gamma$ the factors $a^S$ and $\psi_r$ assign $a$ to the index
$k-1$ (it is the suffix if $n=0$, and otherwise it is fresh and exceeds
$c_1$), $a$ to $c_n$ if $n\geq1$, and to every other fresh index $p$ of
$\gamma$ the factor $a$ or $t^{-1}_{\xi(p)}$ according as the next
index is smaller or larger than $p$. The latter is the status factor of
$\xi(p)$ in $\operatorname{end}(c)$: the label with lower index $p$
moves $\xi(p)$ to position $k$ or $k-1$, the next label moves it to
the position of the next index, and it stays there because no index is
repeated in a tame chain; for $p=a_m$ the next index is $k-1>a_m$ and
$\xi(a_m)$ ends at position $k$, Out. The indices of $F'$ outside
$\gamma$ are fixed, and $k-1$ is in $\gamma$. Therefore $\Sigma(\gamma)=t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,a\beta\,\widehat C_F(\mu)$
if $n=0$ and $\Sigma(\gamma)=t^{-1}_{v(k)}\,a\beta\,\widehat C^\flat_F(c)$
if $n\geq1$, where $v=\operatorname{end}(\gamma)=\operatorname{end}(c)t_{k-1,k}$,
and since the edge $\operatorname{end}(c)\to v$ is Bruhat in the clean
frame,
\[
 \sum_vA^F_{\xi,v}\G_v=\Theta\bigl[(t^\ast-a)\,\mathcal U'\mathcal Q_\varnothing+a\beta\,E\,\mathcal Q\bigr].
\]
Comparing with \eqref{eq:M-operator}, the block identity reads
\[
 \mathcal U'\bigl[(1-B)(t^\ast-a)\mathcal Q_\varnothing+a(1-B)\beta E\Pi_t\mathcal P+aB(1-\beta E)\Pi_w\mathcal P\bigr]
 =(1-B)\bigl[(t^\ast-a)\mathcal U'\mathcal Q_\varnothing+a\beta E\mathcal Q\bigr].
\]
Cancelling the terms with $\mathcal Q_\varnothing$, dividing by $a\beta$
and applying $\mathcal K=\mathcal U'^{-1}$ \eqref{eq:UK} gives
$(1-B)E\Pi_t\mathcal P+\beta q_{k-1}(1-\beta E)\Pi_w\mathcal P=(1-B)\mathcal KE\mathcal Q$,
as $B/\beta=\beta q_{k-1}$. Now $1-B=(1-\beta E)(1+\beta E)$ and
$1-\beta E$ is invertible, with inverse $(1+\beta E)/(1-B)$, because
$E^2=q_{k-1}$. Cancelling $1-\beta E$ leaves
$(1+\beta E)E\Pi_t\mathcal P+\beta q_{k-1}\Pi_w\mathcal P=(1+\beta E)\mathcal KE\mathcal Q$,
and $(1+\beta E)E=E+\beta q_{k-1}$ turns this into \eqref{eq:chain-form}.
\end{proof}

Identity \eqref{eq:chain-form} involves chains only: on the left the
tame chains followed by the edge $(k-1,k)$ and all chains with the
factor $\beta q_{k-1}$, on the right the chains of $\mathcal Q$
followed by $(k-1,k)$, by a strictly increasing sequence of labels
$(c,k)$, $c\leq k-2$, and optionally by $(k-1,k)$ again.

\paragraph{No fresh position below $k-1$.}
Assume now $F=\{k-1\}$, so that $F'=\varnothing$ and no status factor
appears below $k-1$. Condition~4 forbids every descent among lower
indices $\leq k-2$, so the level-$(k-2)$ block chains are the directed
words $\mu\nu$ with $\mu=(a_1,k)\cdots(a_m,k)$, $a_1<\cdots<a_m$, and
$\nu=(c_1,k-1)\cdots(c_n,k-1)$, $c_1<\cdots<c_n$, where $\mu$ and $\nu$
may share lower indices; we identify such a chain with the pair
$(\mu,\nu)$ of increasing sequences, call it \emph{shared} if
$\mu\cap\nu\neq\varnothing$, and note that shared chains are wild.
With $\mathcal L=(1+\beta L_{k-2})\cdots(1+\beta L_1)$ we have
$\mathcal Q_\varnothing=\mathcal K\G_\xi$ and
$\mathcal P=\mathcal L\mathcal K\G_\xi$. The level-$(k-1)$ block
chains are the words $\mu$ and the words $\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$ with
$\mu\cap\nu=\varnothing$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:A-closed} the only
ones with a fresh suffix are $\varnothing$ and the words
$\mu(k-1,k)$; hence
$\sum_vA^F_{\xi,v}\G_v=\Theta\bigl[(t^\ast-a)\G_\xi+a\beta E\mathcal K\G_\xi\bigr]$.
For a pair $(\mu,\nu)$ with $|\nu|=n$ and $0\leq l\leq n$ put
\[
 Z_l(\mu,\nu)=P[\mu\,\nu_{\leq l}\,(k-1,k)\,\tilde\nu_{>l}]\,\G_\xi,
 \qquad
 W_l(\mu,\nu)=P[\mu\,(c_l,k)\,\nu_{\leq l}\,\tilde\nu_{>l}]\,\G_\xi\quad(l\geq1),
\]
where $\nu_{\leq l}$ consists of the first $l$ labels of $\nu$ and
$\tilde\nu_{>l}$ of the remaining ones with upper index $k$. Thus
$Z_0(\mu,\nu)=P[\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu]\G_\xi$ is the braided word and
$Z_n(\mu,\nu)=E\,P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$ is the chain followed by $(k-1,k)$.

\begin{lemma}[Telescoping]\label{lem:telescoping}
For every pair $(\mu,\nu)$ and $1\leq l\leq n$,
$Z_l(\mu,\nu)-Z_{l-1}(\mu,\nu)=W_l(\mu,\nu)$. Consequently exactly one
of the following holds: all $W_l$ vanish and $Z_0=Z_n$; or $Z_0=0$,
$Z_n\neq0$, and exactly one $W_l$ is nonzero, equal to $Z_n$; or
$Z_0=Z_n=0$ and all $W_l$ vanish.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $c=c_l$. By \eqref{eq:ybe-C} with $(a,b,c)=(c,k-1,k)$,
$EL_c=K_cE+L_cK_c$, and $K_c$ commutes with $L_{c'}$ for $c'\neq c$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:ybe}(1). Hence
\begin{align*}
 Z_l&=P[\tilde\nu_{>l}]\,E\,L_c\,P[\nu_{<l}]P[\mu]\G_\xi\\
 &=P[\tilde\nu_{>l}]K_cE\,P[\nu_{<l}]P[\mu]\G_\xi
  +P[\tilde\nu_{>l}]L_c\,P[\nu_{<l}]\,K_c\,P[\mu]\G_\xi
 =Z_{l-1}+W_l .
\end{align*}
Each of $Z_0$, $Z_n$, $W_l$ is zero or a single monomial in $q$ times
a basis vector, and $Z_0$, $Z_n$ have the same endpoint; so in
$Z_n=Z_0+\sum_lW_l$ a nonzero $Z_0$ forces all $W_l$ to vanish, and a
nonzero $Z_n$ with $Z_0=0$ forces exactly one $W_l$ to be nonzero.
\end{proof}

The proof uses only the Yang--Baxter relations, so the lemma and its
proof apply verbatim to arbitrary words $\mu=(a_1,k)\cdots(a_m,k)$ and
$\nu=(c_1,k-1)\cdots(c_n,k-1)$ with $c_1,\ldots,c_n$ pairwise
distinct, with $Z_l$ and $W_l$ defined by the same formulas; we shall
use it in this generality for general $F$.

For a pair that is not shared the lemma recovers
Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}: $Z_0=Z_n$ if the chain is tame and
$Z_0=0$ if it is wild. For shared pairs it gives the new information
that a directed braided word has the same weight as the chain followed
by $(k-1,k)$. Let $\mathcal W$ be the set of directed wild pairs, and
split it as $\mathcal W=\mathcal W_A\sqcup\mathcal W_B$, where
$\mathcal W_B$ consists of the shared pairs with $Z_0\neq0$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:F-minimal}
Let $F=\{k-1\}$. The block identity \eqref{eq:block} for $(\xi,F)$ is
equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:star}
 \sum_{(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B}\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}P[\mu\nu]\,\G_\xi
 =\beta E\sum_{(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A}\beta^{|\mu'|+|\nu'|}P[\mu'\nu']\,\G_\xi .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Here $\mathcal Q=\mathcal Q_\varnothing=\mathcal K\G_\xi$ and
$\mathcal P=\mathcal L\mathcal K\G_\xi$, so by
Proposition~\ref{prop:chain-form} the block identity is equivalent to
\[
 E\mathcal P_t+\beta q_{k-1}(\mathcal P_t+\mathcal P_w)=(1+\beta E)\,\mathcal KE\mathcal K\G_\xi,
\]
where $\mathcal P_t=\Pi_t\mathcal P$ and $\mathcal P_w=\Pi_w\mathcal P$
are the tame and wild parts. Let
$\mathcal R=\sum\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}Z_0(\mu,\nu)$ over the shared
pairs. Since $\mathcal K E\mathcal K\G_\xi=\sum_{\mu,\nu}\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}Z_0(\mu,\nu)$
over all pairs, and the non-shared pairs contribute
$E\mathcal P_t$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:braid-bijection}, we have
$\mathcal K E\mathcal K\G_\xi=E\mathcal P_t+\mathcal R$. Substituting,
the right side becomes
$E\mathcal P_t+\mathcal R+\beta E(E\mathcal P_t+\mathcal R)
 =E\mathcal P_t+\beta q_{k-1}\mathcal P_t+(1+\beta E)\mathcal R$,
so the block identity is equivalent to
$(1+\beta E)\mathcal R=\beta q_{k-1}\mathcal P_w$. Now
$\mathcal R=\sum_{\mathcal W_B}\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}Z_0(\mu,\nu)$, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping} $Z_0=Z_n=E\,P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$ on
$\mathcal W_B$, so
$\beta E\mathcal R=\beta q_{k-1}\sum_{\mathcal W_B}\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$
and $\mathcal R=E\sum_{\mathcal W_B}\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$.
Since $\mathcal P_w$ is the sum of $\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$
over $\mathcal W_A\sqcup\mathcal W_B$, cancelling the $\mathcal W_B$
part and applying the invertible operator $E^{-1}=q_{k-1}^{-1}E$ gives
\eqref{eq:star}.
\end{proof}

Identity \eqref{eq:star} says that the wild chains with a directed
braided word are, up to the edge $(k-1,k)$ and a factor $\beta$,
exactly the remaining wild chains. We now describe both families
explicitly and match them. The description rests on the following
elementary observation.

\paragraph{Right-to-left maxima.}

\begin{lemma}[Increasing chains are right-to-left maxima]\label{lem:rlmax}
Let $z$ be an arrangement of distinct values at the positions
$1,\ldots,b$ in which $z(b)$ is the largest value, and let
$a_1<\cdots<a_m<b$. The word $(a_1,b)(a_2,b)\cdots(a_m,b)$ is a
directed path from $z$ in the quantum Bruhat graph if and only if
every $a_i$ is a right-to-left maximum of $z$ on the positions
$1,\ldots,b-1$, that is, $z(a_i)>z(p)$ for all $a_i<p<b$. In that
case all its edges are Bruhat, position $b$ carries the values
$z(a_1)>z(a_2)>\cdots>z(a_m)$ in turn, and position $a_i$ receives
$z(a_{i-1})$, where $z(a_0):=z(b)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induction on $m$, the case $m=0$ being empty. Suppose the first $m-1$
labels form a directed path, that $a_1,\ldots,a_{m-1}$ are
right-to-left maxima and that the edges are as described; so just
before the label $(a_m,b)$ position $b$ carries $z(a_{m-1})$, position
$a_m$ still carries $z(a_m)$, and the positions strictly between $a_m$
and $b$, none of which is an $a_i$, carry their original values. If
$m\geq2$, these values are smaller than $z(a_{m-1})$, because
$a_{m-1}<a_m$ is a right-to-left maximum, and so is $z(a_m)$; a quantum
edge $(a_m,b)$ would require $z(a_m)>z(a_{m-1})$. If $m=1$, position
$b$ carries the largest value. So the edge $(a_m,b)$, if it exists, is
Bruhat, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:arc} it exists if and only if no value
at a position strictly between $a_m$ and $b$ lies in
$(z(a_m),z(a_{m-1}))$; as these values are below $z(a_{m-1})$, this
says that they are below $z(a_m)$, that is, $a_m$ is a right-to-left
maximum. The edge then moves $z(a_m)$ to $b$ and $z(a_{m-1})$ to $a_m$.
\end{proof}

We apply this to the pairs $(\mu,\nu)$. Let
\[
 R_\xi=\{p\leq k-2:\ \xi(p)>\xi(p')\text{ for all }p<p'\leq k-1\}
\]
be the set of right-to-left maxima of $\xi$ on $\{1,\ldots,k-1\}$
other than $k-1$ itself; their values exceed $c^\ast$. Let
$H=\{p\leq k-2:\xi(p)<c^\ast\}$, let $H_{\max}$ be the set of
right-to-left maxima of $\xi$ restricted to $H$, that is, the $p\in H$
with $\xi(p)>\xi(p')$ for all $p'\in H$ with $p'>p$, and let
$p_H=\max H$, with $p_H=0$ if $H=\varnothing$. For a word $\mu$ with
lower indices $a_1<\cdots<a_m$ put $a_m=0$ if $m=0$ and
\[
 R_\mu=H_{\max}\ \cup\ \{a\in\mu:\ a>p_H\}\ \cup\ \{r\in R_\xi:\ r>a_m,\ r>p_H\}.
\]
We identify $\mu$ and $\nu$ with their sets of lower indices, and
split $\nu=\nu^H\nu^L$, where $\nu^H$ consists of the labels of $\nu$
with lower index $\leq p_H$ and $\nu^L$ of those with lower index
$>p_H$; as $\nu$ is increasing, $\nu^H$ precedes $\nu^L$.

\begin{lemma}[Directed pairs and their braided words]\label{lem:directed-pairs}
Let $F=\{k-1\}$ and work in the clean frame.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The chain $\mu\nu$ is directed if and only if $\mu\subseteq R_\xi$
 and $\nu\subseteq R_\mu$.
\item Let $\mu\nu$ be directed. Along $\mu$ the value at position $k$
 runs through $e_0=k>e_1>\cdots>e_m$, $e_i=\xi(a_i)$, position $a_i$
 receives $e_{i-1}$, and $e_m>c^\ast$. Along $\nu$ the value at
 position $k-1$ starts at $c^\ast$, then runs through the values
 $\xi(c)$, $c\in\nu^H$, which are smaller than $c^\ast$ and decrease,
 and then through the values of $\nu^L$, which exceed $c^\ast$ and
 decrease: the value of $c=a_i\in\nu^L\cap\mu$ is $e_{i-1}>e_m$, and
 the value of $c\in\nu^L\setminus\mu$ is $\xi(c)\in(c^\ast,e_m)$. In
 particular $\mu\nu$ is wild if and only if $\nu^L\neq\varnothing$, and
 it is shared if and only if $\nu^L\cap\mu\neq\varnothing$.
\item $Z_0(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ if and only if $\mu\nu$ is directed and
 $\nu^L\subseteq\mu$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) The word $\mu$ is a directed path from $\xi$ if and only if
$\mu\subseteq R_\xi$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:rlmax} with $z=\xi$ and $b=k$.
Let $x_\mu$ be its endpoint and $y=y_\mu$ the restriction of $x_\mu$
to the positions $1,\ldots,k-2$; thus $y(a_i)=e_{i-1}$ and $y(p)=\xi(p)$
for $p\notin\mu$, and $x_\mu(k-1)=c^\ast$. The labels of $\nu$
involve only the positions $1,\ldots,k-1$. Rotate by $\rho_{c^\ast}$;
by Corollary~\ref{cor:cyclic} $\nu$ is directed from $x_\mu$ if and
only if it is directed from $\rho_{c^\ast}x_\mu$, where $c^\ast$ has
become the largest value and sits at position $k-1$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:rlmax} with $b=k-1$, $\nu$ is directed if and only if
its lower indices are right-to-left maxima of $\rho_{c^\ast}y$. In
the rotated order every value below $c^\ast$ exceeds every value above
$c^\ast$, and $\mu\cap H=\varnothing$ since the values of $\mu$ exceed
$c^\ast$. Hence a position $p\in H$ is a right-to-left maximum of
$\rho_{c^\ast}y$ if and only if $\xi(p)>\xi(p')$ for all $p'\in H$
with $p'>p$, that is, $p\in H_{\max}$; and a position $p\notin H$ is
one if and only if $p>p_H$ and $y(p)>y(p')$ for all $p<p'\leq k-2$,
that is, $p$ is a right-to-left maximum of $y$ in the usual order.
The latter are the positions $a_i\in\mu$---the values to the right of
$a_i$ are $e_{i'-1}\leq e_i$ at the $a_{i'}$ with $i'>i$ and
$\xi(p')<\xi(a_i)=e_i$ elsewhere, since $a_i\in R_\xi$---and the
positions $p\notin\mu$ with $p>a_m$ and $p\in R_\xi$: for $p>a_m$ the
arrangement $y$ agrees with $\xi$ at all positions $\geq p$, so $p$ is
a right-to-left maximum of $y$ if and only if $\xi(p)>\xi(p')$ for
all $p<p'\leq k-2$, which together with $\xi(p)>c^\ast=\xi(k-1)$ says
$p\in R_\xi$; and for $p\notin\mu$ with $p<a_m$, let $i$ be minimal
with $a_i>p$: then $y(a_i)=e_{i-1}$ exceeds $\xi(p)$, because
$e_0=k$, and for $i\geq2$ the position $a_{i-1}<p$ is a right-to-left
maximum of $\xi$. Hence the right-to-left maxima of $\rho_{c^\ast}y$
are exactly the elements of $R_\mu$.

(2) The statements about $\mu$ are Lemma~\ref{lem:rlmax}; $e_m>c^\ast$
because position $k-1$ lies strictly between every $a_i$ and $k$, so
the cover conditions give $c^\ast\notin(e_i,e_{i-1})$ for all $i$,
whence $c^\ast<e_m$ by induction. Along $\nu$ the value at $k-1$ is
replaced by $y(c)$ at the label $(c,k-1)$, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:rlmax} in the rotated frame these values strictly
decrease in the rotated order $c^\ast+1<\cdots<k<1<\cdots<c^\ast$;
the values of $\nu^H\subseteq H$ are $\xi(c)<c^\ast$ and the values of
$\nu^L$ are $y(c)>c^\ast$, namely $e_{i-1}$ for $c=a_i\in\mu$, and
$\xi(c)$ for $c\in R_\xi\setminus\mu$ with $c>a_m$, where
$c^\ast<\xi(c)<\xi(a_m)=e_m$ because $a_m<c$ are both right-to-left
maxima of $\xi$. The final value at $k-1$ exceeds $c^\ast$ exactly when
$\nu^L\neq\varnothing$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:wild-char} gives the
characterization of wild chains, and $\nu\cap\mu=\nu^L\cap\mu$ since
$\mu\cap H=\varnothing$.

(3) Compare the words $\mu\nu$ and $\mu(k-1,k)\tilde\nu$. After
$\mu(k-1,k)$ the positions $1,\ldots,k-2$ carry $y$, position $k-1$
carries $e_m$ and position $k$ carries $c^\ast$. Suppose both words are
directed up to their $j$th label of $\nu$, respectively $\tilde\nu$,
exclusive, and write $f_0=c^\ast$ and $f_i=y(c_i)$ for the values that
position $k-1$ takes along $\nu$. The $j$th label $(c_j,k-1)$ of $\nu$
exchanges the value $f_j$ at $c_j$, a position not touched by the
earlier labels, with the value $f_{j-1}$ at $k-1$; the $j$th label
$(c_j,k)$ of $\tilde\nu$ exchanges the same two values between the
positions $c_j$ and $k$. The positions strictly between are the same,
with the same values, except that $k-1$, carrying $e_m$, is
intermediate for the second label only. By Lemma~\ref{lem:arc} the
second label is an edge if and only if the first one is and
$e_m\notin\operatorname{arc}(f_j,f_{j-1})$. Hence, by induction on
$j$, $Z_0(\mu,\nu)\neq0$
if and only if $\mu\nu$ is directed and
$e_m\notin\operatorname{arc}(f_j,f_{j-1})$ for $j=1,\ldots,n$. We
evaluate these conditions using (2). For a label of $\nu^H$ we have
$f_j<f_{j-1}\leq c^\ast<e_m$, and the arc is $(f_j,f_{j-1})$, which
does not contain $e_m$. For the first label of $\nu^L$ we have
$f_{j-1}\leq c^\ast<f_j$, and the arc $\{f_j+1,\ldots,k\}\cup\{1,\ldots,f_{j-1}-1\}$
avoids $e_m$ if and only if $f_j>e_m$. For a later label of $\nu^L$ we
have $c^\ast<f_j<f_{j-1}$ and, inductively, $f_{j-1}>e_m$, so the arc
$(f_j,f_{j-1})$ avoids $e_m$ if and only if $f_j>e_m$. Thus
$Z_0(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ if and only if every value of $\nu^L$ exceeds
$e_m$, which by (2) means $\nu^L\subseteq\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The two families]\label{prop:families}
Let $F=\{k-1\}$, in the clean frame.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathcal W_B$ is the set of pairs with $\mu\subseteq R_\xi$,
 $\nu^H\subseteq H_{\max}$ and $\varnothing\neq\nu^L\subseteq\{a\in\mu:a>p_H\}$,
 and $\mathcal W_A$ is the set of pairs with $\mu\subseteq R_\xi$,
 $\nu\subseteq R_\mu$ and $\nu^L\not\subseteq\mu$.
\item For $(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$ let $j=\max\nu$, which lies in
 $\mu$, and put
 \[
  \Phi(\mu,\nu)=\bigl(\mu_{<j},\ \nu\cup\mu_{>j}\bigr),
 \]
 where $\mu_{<j}$, $\mu_{>j}$ are the parts of $\mu$ below and above
 $j$. For $(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A$ let $U=\nu'^L\setminus\mu'$,
 which is nonempty, and put
 \[
  \Psi(\mu',\nu')=\bigl(\mu'\cup U,\ \nu'^H\cup(\nu'^L\cap\mu')\cup\{\min U\}\bigr).
 \]
 Then $\Phi\colon\mathcal W_B\to\mathcal W_A$ and
 $\Psi\colon\mathcal W_A\to\mathcal W_B$ are mutually inverse
 bijections, $|\Phi(\mu,\nu)|=|\mu|+|\nu|-1$, and
 \[
  P[\mu\nu]\,\G_\xi=E\,P[\Phi(\mu,\nu)]\,\G_\xi
  \qquad\text{for all }(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B .
 \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) By Lemma~\ref{lem:directed-pairs}, a pair is in $\mathcal W_B$
(directed, shared, $Z_0\neq0$) if and only if $\mu\subseteq R_\xi$,
$\nu\subseteq R_\mu$, $\nu^L\subseteq\mu$ and $\nu^L\cap\mu\neq\varnothing$;
here $\nu^H\subseteq R_\mu\cap H=H_{\max}$, and
$\nu^L\subseteq R_\mu\cap\mu=\{a\in\mu:a>p_H\}$. A pair is in
$\mathcal W_A$ (directed, wild, $Z_0=0$) if and only if
$\mu\subseteq R_\xi$, $\nu\subseteq R_\mu$, $\nu^L\neq\varnothing$ and
$\nu^L\not\subseteq\mu$, and the third condition follows from the
fourth.

(2) Let $(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$ and $(\mu',\nu')=\Phi(\mu,\nu)$.
Since $\nu^L\subseteq\mu$ is nonempty, $j=\max\nu=\max\nu^L\in\mu$ and
$j>p_H$. We have $\mu'=\mu_{<j}\subseteq R_\xi$, $\nu'^H=\nu^H\subseteq H_{\max}$
and $\nu'^L=\nu^L_{<j}\cup\{j\}\cup\mu_{>j}$. Here
$\nu^L_{<j}\subseteq\{a\in\mu':a>p_H\}\subseteq R_{\mu'}$, and
$\{j\}\cup\mu_{>j}$ consists of elements of $R_\xi$ exceeding
$\max\mu'$ and $p_H$, so it lies in $R_{\mu'}$ as well. Thus
$\nu'\subseteq R_{\mu'}$, and $j\in\nu'^L\setminus\mu'$; by (1),
$(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A$. Clearly $|\mu'|+|\nu'|=|\mu|+|\nu|-1$.

Let $(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A$ and $(\mu,\nu)=\Psi(\mu',\nu')$. The
elements of $U=\nu'^L\setminus\mu'\subseteq R_{\mu'}\setminus\mu'$ lie
in $R_\xi$ and exceed $\max\mu'$ and $p_H$, so $\mu=\mu'\cup U\subseteq R_\xi$,
and $\mu'<U$ elementwise. Further $\nu^H=\nu'^H\subseteq H_{\max}$ and
$\nu^L=(\nu'^L\cap\mu')\cup\{\min U\}$ is a nonempty subset of $\mu$
all of whose elements exceed $p_H$. By (1), $(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$.

The two maps are inverse to each other. Starting from
$(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$ with $j=\max\nu$, the pair
$(\mu',\nu')=\Phi(\mu,\nu)$ has $\nu'^L\setminus\mu'=\{j\}\cup\mu_{>j}$
with minimum $j$ and $\nu'^L\cap\mu'=\nu^L_{<j}$, so
$\Psi(\mu',\nu')=(\mu_{<j}\cup\{j\}\cup\mu_{>j},\ \nu^H\cup\nu^L_{<j}\cup\{j\})=(\mu,\nu)$.
Starting from $(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A$ with $c=\min U$, the pair
$(\mu,\nu)=\Psi(\mu',\nu')$ has $\max\nu=c$, since $\nu'^L\cap\mu'\subseteq\mu'<U$,
so $\mu_{<c}=\mu'$ and $\mu_{>c}=U\setminus\{c\}$, and
$\Phi(\mu,\nu)=(\mu',\ \nu'^H\cup(\nu'^L\cap\mu')\cup U)=(\mu',\nu')$.

For the identity of weights, let $(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$,
$j=\max\nu$ and $(\mu',\nu')=\Phi(\mu,\nu)$. The label $j$ is the
$l_0$th label of $\nu'=\nu_{<j}\,(j,k-1)\,\overline{\mu_{>j}}$, where
the bar converts upper indices $k$ to $k-1$ and $l_0=|\nu_{<j}|+1$. Using
Lemma~\ref{lem:ybe}(1) to move the operators $K_a$, $a>j$, of
$\mu_{>j}$ past $L_j$ and past the $L_c$, $c<j$, of $\nu_{<j}$, and to
move $K_j$ past the latter,
\[
 P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi=P[\mu_{>j}]\,L_j\,P[\nu_{<j}]\,K_j\,P[\mu_{<j}]\G_\xi
 =W_{l_0}(\mu',\nu').
\]
As $(\mu,\nu)$ is directed, $W_{l_0}(\mu',\nu')\neq0$, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping} gives $Z_0(\mu',\nu')=0$ and
$E\,P[\mu'\nu']\G_\xi=Z_{|\nu'|}(\mu',\nu')=W_{l_0}(\mu',\nu')=P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$.
\end{proof}

The proof shows, in passing, that for $(\mu',\nu')\in\mathcal W_A$
the unique nonzero defect $W_{l}(\mu',\nu')$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping} occurs at the first label of $\nu'$ whose
lower index exceeds $p_H$ and is not in $\mu'$.

\begin{theorem}[The block identity when only position $k-1$ is fresh]\label{thm:F-minimal}
The block identity \eqref{eq:block} holds for $F=\{k-1\}$ and every
$\xi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the conventions of this subsection we may assume the clean frame.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:families}, summing
$\beta^{|\mu|+|\nu|}P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi=\beta E\,\beta^{|\Phi(\mu,\nu)|}P[\Phi(\mu,\nu)]\G_\xi$
over $(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal W_B$ and using that $\Phi$ is a bijection
onto $\mathcal W_A$ gives \eqref{eq:star}, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:F-minimal} gives \eqref{eq:block}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{General $F$: the components of the chain form.}
We return to arbitrary $F\ni k-1$ and record what the chain form
\eqref{eq:chain-form} looks like component by component. Write
$s_p=t^{-1}_{\xi(p)}-a$ for $p\in F'$. The status factor of $\xi(p)$
at a chain endpoint is $s_p$, $a$ or $s_p+a$ according as $\xi(p)$ is
fixed, Left or Out, and in $\widehat C^\flat_F$ the factor of
$\xi(c_n)$ is $a$. Expanding the products over $F'$, both sides of
\eqref{eq:chain-form} become sums of monomials $s^Sa^{|F'\setminus S|}$,
$S\subseteq F'$, with coefficients in the span of the
$q^D\beta^m\G_v$; the operators $E$ and $\mathcal K$ do not involve
$s$ or $a$. Hence \eqref{eq:chain-form} is equivalent to the family of
identities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-form-S}
 E\,\Pi_t\mathcal P^0_S+\beta q_{k-1}\mathcal P^0_S
 =(1+\beta E)\,\mathcal KE\bigl(\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,S}+\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,S}\bigr),
 \qquad S\subseteq F',
\end{equation}
in which $\mathcal P^0_S$ and $\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,S}$ are the
sums of $\beta^{|c|}q^{D(c)}\G_{\operatorname{end}(c)}$ over the
chains $c$, respectively the chains $c=\mu$, whose endpoint has no Left
value $\xi(p)$ with $p\in S$ and no fixed value $\xi(p)$ with
$p\in F'\setminus S$, and $\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,S}$ is the same sum
over the tame chains with $c_n\in F'\setminus S$ for which the same
holds when $\xi(c_n)$ is counted as Left. We call such chains
\emph{$S$-admissible}. These identities contain neither $a$ nor the
equivariant parameters.

\begin{lemma}[Fresh descents leave a value Left]\label{lem:descent-left}
Let $c=\mu\nu$ be a level-$(k-2)$ block chain with a descent, that is,
two consecutive labels $(a_i,k)(a_{i+1},k)$ with $a_i>a_{i+1}$ or
$(c_j,k-1)(c_{j+1},k-1)$ with $c_j>c_{j+1}$. Then some fresh value
$\xi(p)$, $p\in F'$, is Left in $\operatorname{end}(c)$. More
precisely, a descent of $\nu$ at $c_j$ leaves $\xi(c_j)$ Left, and a
descent of $\mu$ at $a_i$ leaves $\xi(a_i)$ Left unless $\nu$ contains
the label $(a_{i+1},k-1)$ preceded by a label with a larger lower
index.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Work in the clean frame. A descent $c_j>c_{j+1}$ of $\nu$ is allowed
only if $c_j\in F'\setminus\mu$; the label $(c_j,k-1)$ moves $\xi(c_j)$
to position $k-1$ and the next label moves it to position
$c_{j+1}<c_j$, which no later label touches. A descent $a_i>a_{i+1}$
of $\mu$ is allowed only if $a_i\in F'$; the value $e_i=\xi(a_i)$ ends
at position $a_{i+1}<a_i$ after the label $(a_{i+1},k)$ and is moved
again only if $\nu$ contains $(a_{i+1},k-1)$, say as its $j$th label.
Suppose that all labels of $\nu$ before this one have lower indices
smaller than $a_{i+1}$. Then at the $j$th label the positions $a_{i+1}$
and $a_i$ still carry $e_i$ and $e_{i-1}$, and $a_i$ lies strictly
between $a_{i+1}$ and $k-1$. The label exchanges $e_i$ with the value
$f_{j-1}$ at position $k-1$; a quantum edge would need
$e_{i-1}\in(f_{j-1},e_i)$, which is absurd, so the edge is Bruhat
with $e_i<f_{j-1}$ and $e_{i-1}\notin(e_i,f_{j-1})$, that is,
$f_{j-1}\in(e_i,e_{i-1})$. We show by descending induction that
$f_{j'}\in(e_i,e_{i-1})$ for all $j'<j$, which is absurd for $j'=0$
because $f_0=c^\ast<e_m\leq e_i$. Let $1\leq j'<j$ with
$f_{j'}\in(e_i,e_{i-1})$. The value $f_{j'}$ is the value at position
$c_{j'}$ after $\mu$; it is not one of the $e_h$, so $c_{j'}\notin\mu$
and $f_{j'}=\xi(c_{j'})$. The cover condition of the $i$th label of
$\mu$ says that no position in $(a_i,k-1]$ other than
$a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1}$ carries a value in $(e_i,e_{i-1})$, so
$c_{j'}<a_i$; also $c_{j'}<a_{i+1}$ by assumption. Hence the
positions $a_{i+1}$ and $a_i$, carrying $e_i$ and $e_{i-1}$, lie
strictly between $c_{j'}$ and $k-1$ at the $j'$th label. A quantum
edge would need $e_{i-1}\in(f_{j'-1},f_{j'})$, contradicting
$f_{j'}<e_{i-1}$; so the edge is Bruhat, $f_{j'}<f_{j'-1}$, and
$e_{i-1}\notin(f_{j'},f_{j'-1})$ forces $f_{j'-1}<e_{i-1}$, that is,
$f_{j'-1}\in(e_i,e_{i-1})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:top-component}
For every $F\ni k-1$ and every $\xi$, the component $S=F'$ of
\eqref{eq:chain-form-S} holds.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $S=F'$ the $S$-admissible chains are those without a Left fresh
value, and $\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,F'}=0$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:descent-left} the $S$-admissible chains have no
descent, that is, $\mu$ and $\nu$ are increasing; conversely an
increasing chain never moves a value to a smaller position. Hence
$\mathcal P^0_{F'}=\mathcal L\mathcal K\G_\xi$ and
$\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,F'}=\mathcal K\G_\xi$, and the component is
the identity \eqref{eq:chain-form} for $(\xi,\{k-1\})$, which holds by
Theorem~\ref{thm:F-minimal} and Proposition~\ref{prop:chain-form}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Chains with descents.}
We now drop the assumption $F=\{k-1\}$. For the rest of this
subsection $c=\mu\nu$ denotes a level-$(k-2)$ block chain for
$(\xi,F)$ in the clean frame, with $\mu=(a_1,k)\cdots(a_m,k)$ and
$\nu=(c_1,k-1)\cdots(c_n,k-1)$. Since the labels are distinct, the
lower indices $a_1,\ldots,a_m$ are pairwise distinct, and so are
$c_1,\ldots,c_n$; condition~4 allows a descent $a_i>a_{i+1}$ only when
$a_i\in F'$, and a descent $c_j>c_{j+1}$ only when $c_j\in F'$ and
$c_j\notin\mu$. We write $e_0=k$ and $e_i=\xi(a_i)$, let $y$ be the
endpoint of $\mu$, and put $f_0=c^\ast$ and $f_j=y(c_j)$; as before we
also write $\mu$ and $\nu$ for the sets of lower indices. The vectors
$Z_l(\mu,\nu)$ and $W_l(\mu,\nu)$ are defined by the same formulas as
before, and Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping} applies to them.

\begin{lemma}[Shape of a block chain]\label{lem:shape}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Every edge of $\mu$ is Bruhat. Along $\mu$ the value at position
 $k$ runs through $e_0>e_1>\cdots>e_m$; we have $y(a_i)=e_{i-1}$,
 $y(p)=\xi(p)$ for $p\notin\mu$, $y(k-1)=c^\ast$ and $y(k)=e_m$. For
 every $i$, no position $p$ with $a_i<p\leq k-1$ other than
 $a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1}$ satisfies $\xi(p)\in(e_i,e_{i-1})$; in particular
 $c^\ast<e_m$.
\item There is $j_0\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}$ such that
 $c^\ast=f_0>f_1>\cdots>f_{j_0-1}$ and
 $f_{j_0}>f_{j_0+1}>\cdots>f_n>c^\ast$. The $j_0$th edge of $\nu$ is
 quantum, all other edges of $\nu$ are Bruhat, and $c$ is wild if and
 only if $j_0\leq n$. If $c_j\in\mu$, say $c_j=a_i$, then
 $f_j=e_{i-1}>e_m$ and $j\geq j_0$; if $c_j\notin\mu$, then
 $f_j=\xi(c_j)$. In particular $c_j\notin\mu$ for $j<j_0$.
\item The $j$th label of $\nu$ exchanges the value $f_j$ at position
 $c_j$ with the value $f_{j-1}$ at position $k-1$. If $j\neq j_0$, no
 position strictly between $c_j$ and $k-1$ carries a value in
 $(f_j,f_{j-1})$; if $j=j_0$, every position strictly between
 $c_{j_0}$ and $k-1$ carries a value in $(f_{j_0-1},f_{j_0})$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation}, applied to $\mu$ with $k-1$ in place of
$k$, rotates by $\vartheta=\xi(k)=k$, which is the identity; so every
edge of $\mu$ is Bruhat. A Bruhat edge $(a_i,k)$ replaces the value at
$k$ by the smaller value at $a_i$, which is $\xi(a_i)$ because no
earlier label of $\mu$ has lower index $a_i$, and it leaves the old
value $e_{i-1}$ at $a_i$, where it stays. By Lemma~\ref{lem:arc} the
edge requires that no value at a position strictly between $a_i$ and
$k$ lies in $(e_i,e_{i-1})$; the positions $a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1}$ carry
$e_0,\ldots,e_{i-2}$, which exceed $e_{i-1}$, and every other position
still carries its value in $\xi$. Position $k-1$ is never a lower
index of $\mu$, so $c^\ast\notin(e_i,e_{i-1})$ for all $i$, and
$c^\ast<e_0$ gives $c^\ast<e_m$ by induction on $i$.

(2) By Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation}, applied to $c$ with $k-2$ in place of
$k$ and $\vartheta=\xi(k-1)=c^\ast$, every edge of the rotated chain
is Bruhat, so along $\nu$ the value at position $k-1$ strictly
decreases in the rotated order $c^\ast+1<\cdots<k<1<\cdots<c^\ast$;
this gives the two decreasing runs, and by the last assertion of that
lemma the $j$th edge is quantum exactly when $f_{j-1}\leq c^\ast<f_j$,
that is for $j=j_0$. The chain is wild if and only if $f_n>c^\ast$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:wild-char}), that is if and only if $j_0\leq n$. The
value at position $c_j$ just before the $j$th label of $\nu$ is
$y(c_j)$, because no earlier label of $\nu$ has lower index $c_j$; by
(1) it is $e_{i-1}>e_m>c^\ast$ if $c_j=a_i$, which forces $j\geq j_0$,
and $\xi(c_j)$ if $c_j\notin\mu$.

(3) is Lemma~\ref{lem:arc}: for $j\neq j_0$ the edge is Bruhat with
$f_j<f_{j-1}$, and $\operatorname{arc}(f_j,f_{j-1})=(f_j,f_{j-1})$;
for $j=j_0$ it is quantum with $f_{j_0}>f_{j_0-1}$, and
$\operatorname{arc}(f_{j_0},f_{j_0-1})$ is the complement of
$[f_{j_0-1},f_{j_0}]$.
\end{proof}

For an increasing chain the labels of $\nu$ with $j<j_0$ form
$\nu^H$ and those with $j\geq j_0$ form $\nu^L$. The next lemma
extends to arbitrary chains the fact, contained in
Lemma~\ref{lem:directed-pairs}(2), that the labels of $\nu^L$ with
lower index in $\mu$ carry values above $e_m$ and the others values
below $e_m$, and that the latter come last.

\begin{lemma}[The labels after the quantum edge]\label{lem:after-quantum}
Let $J=\{j:\ c_j\notin\mu\text{ and }c^\ast<\xi(c_j)<e_m\}$. Then
$J=\{j\geq j_0:\ c_j\notin\mu\}=\{j\geq j_0:\ f_j<e_m\}$, and $J$ is a
final segment of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Thus, if $J=\varnothing$, then
$c_j\in\mu$ for every $j\geq j_0$; and if $J\neq\varnothing$ and
$l^\ast=\min J$, then $c_j\in\mu$ for $j_0\leq j<l^\ast$ and
$c_j\notin\mu$ for $j\geq l^\ast$. Moreover, writing $c_j=a_{i(j)}$
for the labels of $\nu$ with lower index in $\mu$, the indices $i(j)$
strictly increase with $j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $c_j\in\mu$ then $j\geq j_0$ and $f_j=e_{i(j)-1}$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(2)); as the $f_j$ with $j\geq j_0$ strictly
decrease, the $i(j)$ strictly increase. For $j\geq j_0$ the value
$f_j$ differs from $e_m=y(k)$, and $f_j<e_m$ forces $c_j\notin\mu$ and
then $f_j=\xi(c_j)\in(c^\ast,e_m)$, so $j\in J$; conversely $j\in J$
gives $f_j=\xi(c_j)>c^\ast$, hence $j\geq j_0$, and $f_j<e_m$. So
$J=\{j\geq j_0:f_j<e_m\}$, which is a final segment because the $f_j$,
$j\geq j_0$, decrease. It remains to show that $c_j\in\mu$ whenever
$j\geq j_0$ and $f_j>e_m$; together with the above this gives
$J=\{j\geq j_0:c_j\notin\mu\}$ and the description of the labels
$c_j$, $j\geq j_0$. Suppose not, and let $j$ be the least index
with $j\geq j_0$, $f_j>e_m$ and $c_j\notin\mu$; then $f_j=\xi(c_j)$,
and $e_m<\xi(c_j)<k=e_0$ gives a unique $h\in\{1,\ldots,m\}$ with
$e_h<\xi(c_j)<e_{h-1}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(1), applied to the
$h$th label of $\mu$ and the position $c_j\notin\mu$, we get
$c_j<a_h$; so $a_h$ lies strictly between $c_j$ and $k-1$ at the $j$th
label of $\nu$. We use Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(3).

If $j=j_0$, every position strictly between $c_{j_0}$ and $k-1$
carries a value smaller than $f_{j_0}=\xi(c_j)$. Position $a_h$ is not
among $c_1,\ldots,c_{j_0-1}$, which are not in $\mu$, so it still
carries $y(a_h)=e_{h-1}>\xi(c_j)$: a contradiction.

If $j>j_0$, then $c_{j-1}\in\mu$ by the minimality of $j$ (as
$f_{j-1}>f_j>e_m$), say $c_{j-1}=a_i$, and $f_{j-1}=e_{i-1}$. No
position strictly between $c_j$ and $k-1$ carries a value in
$(\xi(c_j),e_{i-1})$. From $e_{i-1}>\xi(c_j)>e_h$ we get $i\leq h$. If
$h=i$, then $c_{j-1}=a_h>c_j$ is a descent of $\nu$ at a lower index
of $\mu$, which condition~4 forbids. So $h>i$. If $a_h$ is not among
$c_1,\ldots,c_{j-1}$, it carries $e_{h-1}$, and
$\xi(c_j)<e_{h-1}<e_{i-1}$: a contradiction. If $a_h=c_{j'}$ with
$j'\leq j-1$, then $j'\neq j-1$ because $a_h\neq a_i$, so $j'<j-1$;
and $f_{j'}=e_{h-1}>c^\ast$ gives $j'\geq j_0$, whence
$f_{j'}>f_{j-1}$, that is $e_{h-1}>e_{i-1}$ and $h<i$: a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Types]\label{lem:types}
For $0\leq l\leq n$ we have $Z_l(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ if and only if
$J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,l\}$. Hence $Z_0(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ if and only if
$J=\varnothing$; and if $J\neq\varnothing$ and $l^\ast=\min J$, then
$Z_l(\mu,\nu)=0$ for $l<l^\ast$, $Z_l(\mu,\nu)=E\,P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$ for
$l\geq l^\ast$, and $W_{l^\ast}(\mu,\nu)=E\,P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$ is the only
nonzero $W_l(\mu,\nu)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We compare the words $\mu\nu_{\leq l}(k-1,k)\tilde\nu_{>l}$ and
$\mu\nu$ as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:directed-pairs}(3). After
$\mu\nu_{\leq l}(k-1,k)$, which is directed, the positions
$1,\ldots,k-2$ carry the same values as after $\mu\nu_{\leq l}$, while
position $k-1$ carries $e_m$ and position $k$ carries $f_l$. Suppose
that both words agree in this way just before their $j$th labels
$(c_j,k)$, respectively $(c_j,k-1)$, for some $j>l$. Both labels
exchange the value $f_j$ at position $c_j$ with the value $f_{j-1}$,
which sits at $k$ in the first word and at $k-1$ in the second; the
positions strictly between are the same, with the same values, except
that $k-1$, carrying $e_m$, is intermediate for the first label only.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:arc} the first label is an edge if and only if the
second one is and $e_m\notin\operatorname{arc}(f_j,f_{j-1})$, and then
the two words agree again in the same way. Hence
$Z_l(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ if and only if
$e_m\notin\operatorname{arc}(f_j,f_{j-1})$ for all $j>l$.

We evaluate this condition with Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(2) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:after-quantum}. For $j<j_0$ the arc is
$(f_j,f_{j-1})\subseteq\{1,\ldots,c^\ast-1\}$, which does not contain
$e_m>c^\ast$. For $j=j_0$ the arc is
$\{f_{j_0}+1,\ldots,k\}\cup\{1,\ldots,f_{j_0-1}-1\}$, and since
$e_m>c^\ast\geq f_{j_0-1}$ it contains $e_m$ if and only if
$f_{j_0}<e_m$, that is $j_0\in J$. For $j>j_0$ the arc is
$(f_j,f_{j-1})$, which contains $e_m$ if and only if
$f_j<e_m<f_{j-1}$, that is $j\in J$ and $j-1\notin J$. As $J$ is a
final segment of $\{j_0,\ldots,n\}$, the arc contains $e_m$ exactly
for $j=\min J$. Hence $Z_l\neq0$ if and only if $J=\varnothing$ or
$\min J\leq l$. The remaining assertions follow from
Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping}: $Z_l=Z_0+\sum_{h\leq l}W_h$ with at most
one nonzero $W_h$, which must then be $W_{l^\ast}$, and
$Z_n=E\,P[\mu\nu]\G_\xi$.
\end{proof}

We call $c$ of \emph{type~B} if $J=\varnothing$, that is if its
braided word $Z_0(\mu,\nu)$ is directed, and of \emph{type~A}
otherwise. A tame chain has $j_0=n+1$, hence is of type~B; a chain of
type~A has $J\neq\varnothing$, hence $j_0\leq n$, and is wild. For
increasing chains, type~B means $\nu^L\subseteq\mu$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:types} reduces to Lemma~\ref{lem:directed-pairs}(3).

\begin{lemma}[Chains of type B]\label{lem:typeB}
Let $c$ be of type~B.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $c$ is wild, then $c_j\in\mu$ for every $j\geq j_0$. Thus the
 lower indices that $\nu$ shares with $\mu$ are
 $a_{i(j_0)},\ldots,a_{i(n)}$ with $i(j_0)<\cdots<i(n)$; in particular
 $c_n\in\mu$, and no label of $\nu$ has a lower index $a_i$ with
 $i>i(n)$.
\item If $a_i>a_{i+1}$ is a descent of $\mu$, then $a_{i+1}\notin\nu$,
 and $\xi(a_i)$ is Left in the endpoint of every prefix
 $\mu\nu_{\leq l}$, $0\leq l\leq n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) is Lemma~\ref{lem:after-quantum} with $J=\varnothing$, together
with Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(2) for the labels $j<j_0$.

(2) Suppose $a_{i+1}=c_j$. Then $f_j=y(a_{i+1})=e_i>c^\ast$, so
$j\geq j_0$, the chain is wild, and by (1) every $c_{j'}$ with
$j'\geq j_0$ lies in $\mu$, with $i(j')$ increasing. Position $a_i$
lies strictly between $c_j=a_{i+1}$ and $k-1$. If $j=j_0$, every such
position carries a value smaller than $f_{j_0}=e_i$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(3)); but $a_i$ is not among
$c_1,\ldots,c_{j_0-1}$, which are not in $\mu$, so it carries
$e_{i-1}>e_i$: a contradiction. If $j>j_0$, let $c_{j-1}=a_{i'}$; then
$i'<i(j)=i+1$, so $i'\leq i$, and $f_{j-1}=e_{i'-1}$. If $i'=i$, then
$c_{j-1}=a_i>a_{i+1}=c_j$ is a descent of $\nu$ at a lower index of
$\mu$, which condition~4 forbids; so $i'<i$. No position strictly
between $a_{i+1}$ and $k-1$ carries a value in $(e_i,e_{i'-1})$. If
$a_i$ is not among $c_1,\ldots,c_{j-1}$, it carries $e_{i-1}$, and
$e_i<e_{i-1}<e_{i'-1}$: a contradiction. If $a_i=c_{j''}$ with
$j''<j$, then $i=i(j'')\leq i(j-1)=i'<i$: a contradiction.

Finally, the value $\xi(a_i)=e_i$ is carried to position $k$ by the
label $(a_i,k)$ and to position $a_{i+1}$ by the label $(a_{i+1},k)$,
and no later label of $\mu$ and no label of $\nu$ has lower index
$a_{i+1}$; so in the endpoint of every prefix $\mu\nu_{\leq l}$ it sits
at $a_{i+1}<a_i$, where it is Left.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The right side of the component identity.}
Fix $S\subseteq F'$. Let $\mathcal C(S)$ be the set of $S$-admissible
chains, $\mathcal T(S)$ the set of tame chains in $\mathcal C(S)$, and
$\mathcal W_A(S)$, $\mathcal W_B(S)$ the sets of wild chains in
$\mathcal C(S)$ of type~A, respectively of type~B; so
$\mathcal C(S)=\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_A(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$,
and $\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$ is the set of $S$-admissible
chains of type~B. By an \emph{$S$-prefix} we mean a chain counted in
$\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,S}+\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,S}$: either a
chain $\gamma=\mu$ in $\mathcal C(S)$, or a tame chain
$\gamma=\mu\nu$ with $\nu\neq\varnothing$ and $c_n\in F'\setminus S$
that is $S$-admissible when $\xi(c_n)$ is counted as Left. Expanding
$\mathcal K$,
\[
 \mathcal KE\bigl(\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,S}+\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,S}\bigr)
 =\sum_{\gamma,\nu'}\beta^{|\gamma|+|\nu'|}\,P[\gamma\,(k-1,k)\,\tilde\nu']\,\G_\xi ,
\]
the sum over the $S$-prefixes $\gamma$ and the strictly increasing
sequences $\nu'$ in $\{1,\ldots,k-2\}$, where $\tilde\nu'$ is the word
of the labels $(c',k)$, $c'\in\nu'$, in increasing order.

\begin{proposition}[The right side]\label{prop:right-side}
Let $S\subseteq F'$. For a chain $c=\mu\nu$ of type~B in
$\mathcal C(S)$ let $l_0(c)$ be the largest $j$ such that
$c_j\in F'\setminus S$ and $c_j\notin\mu$, or $l_0(c)=0$ if there is
no such $j$. Then
\[
 c\longmapsto\bigl(\mu\nu_{\leq l_0(c)},\ \nu_{>l_0(c)}\bigr)
\]
is a bijection from $\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$ onto the set
of pairs $(\gamma,\nu')$ of an $S$-prefix $\gamma$ and a strictly
increasing sequence $\nu'$ for which $\gamma(k-1,k)\tilde\nu'$ is
directed, and for these pairs
$P[\gamma(k-1,k)\tilde\nu']\,\G_\xi=E\,P[c]\,\G_\xi$. Consequently
\[
 \mathcal KE\bigl(\mathcal Q^0_{\varnothing,S}+\mathcal P^{\flat,0}_{t,S}\bigr)
 =E\sum_{c\in\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)}\beta^{|c|}P[c]\,\G_\xi .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: the tail avoids the prefix.} Let $\gamma=\mu\nu_{\leq l}$
be an $S$-prefix ($l\geq0$) and $\nu'=(c'_1<\cdots<c'_r)$ a strictly
increasing sequence such that $\omega=\gamma(k-1,k)\tilde\nu'$ is
directed. We claim that no $c'_h$ equals a $c_j$ with $j\leq l$.
Suppose it does, and let $i$ be the least index with $c'_i=c_j$ for
some $j\leq l$; so $l\geq1$. Since $\gamma$ is tame,
$c^\ast=f_0>f_1>\cdots>f_l$. After $\gamma(k-1,k)$ position $k-1$
carries $e_m$, which no label of $\tilde\nu'$ moves, and position $k$
carries $g_0=f_l$; let $g_h$ be the value brought to position $k$ by
the $h$th label of $\tilde\nu'$. Position $k-1$ lies strictly between
$c'_h$ and $k$ for every $h$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:arc}, if the $h$th
label is a quantum edge, then $g_h>g_{h-1}$ and every intermediate
value, in particular $e_m$, lies in $(g_{h-1},g_h)$, so $g_h>e_m$; if
it is a Bruhat edge, then $g_h<g_{h-1}$ and $e_m\notin(g_h,g_{h-1})$,
so $g_h>e_m$ whenever $g_{h-1}>e_m$. Hence once some $g_h$ exceeds
$e_m$, all later ones do. Now consider the $i$th label $(c_j,k)$.
Position $c_j$ carries $f_{j-1}$: the $j$th label of $\nu$ put it
there, and no later label of $\gamma$ or earlier label of $\tilde\nu'$
has lower index $c_j$. Since $f_{j-1}\leq c^\ast<e_m$ and $e_m$ is
intermediate, the label cannot be a quantum edge, so it is a Bruhat
edge with $f_{j-1}<g_{i-1}$ and $e_m\notin(f_{j-1},g_{i-1})$, that is
$g_{i-1}<e_m$. Then no $g_h$ with $h<i$ exceeds $e_m$, so the first
$i-1$ labels of $\tilde\nu'$ are Bruhat edges and
$g_{i-1}\leq g_0=f_l$. But $j\leq l$ gives $f_l<f_{j-1}<g_{i-1}$: a
contradiction.

\emph{Step 2: the word is a chain of type B in $\mathcal C(S)$, cut at
$l_0$.} Keep $\gamma$, $\nu'$, $\omega$ as in Step~1 and let
$c=\mu\nu$ with $\nu=\nu_{\leq l}\nu'$, the labels of $\nu'$ taken
with upper index $k-1$. The word $c$ has pairwise distinct labels by
Step~1, and it satisfies condition~4: inside $\gamma$ and inside the
increasing $\nu'$ there is nothing to check; at the junction, if $l=0$
the upper index drops from $k$ to $k-1$, and if $l\geq1$ and
$c_l>c'_1$, then $c_l\in F'$ by the definition of an $S$-prefix and
$c_l\notin\mu$ because $\gamma$ is tame (by the definition of tame
chains, $\nu_{\leq l}$ shares no lower index with $\mu$), so the
descent is allowed. By Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping},
$Z_n(\mu,\nu)=Z_l(\mu,\nu)+\sum_{h>l}W_h(\mu,\nu)$, where
$Z_l(\mu,\nu)=P[\omega]\G_\xi\neq0$ and every term is zero or a
monomial with coefficient $1$ times a basis vector; no cancellation
can occur among such terms, so
$Z_n(\mu,\nu)=E\,P[c]\G_\xi\neq0$, and $c$ is directed, hence a
level-$(k-2)$ block chain. By Lemma~\ref{lem:types},
$J(c)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,l\}$; if $J(c)\neq\varnothing$ this gives
$l\geq\min J(c)\geq j_0$, so the prefix $\gamma$ would contain the
quantum edge of $\nu$, contradicting its tameness. Hence
$J(c)=\varnothing$, $c$ is of type~B, and
$P[\omega]\G_\xi=Z_l(\mu,\nu)=Z_n(\mu,\nu)=E\,P[c]\G_\xi$.

We compare the statuses of the fresh values $\xi(p)$, $p\in F'$, in
the endpoints of $\gamma$ and of $c$. The labels of $\nu'$ are the
only difference.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $p\notin\mu\cup\nu$, then $\xi(p)$ is fixed in both.
\item If $p=c_l$ (with $l\geq1$), then $p\in F'\setminus S$, and
 $\xi(p)$ is not fixed in either endpoint.
\item If $p=c_j$ with $j<l$, then $p\notin\mu$ by
 Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(2), since $j<j_0$; the value $\xi(p)$ is moved
 to $k-1$ by the $j$th label and to $c_{j+1}$ by the $(j+1)$th, and no
 label of $\nu'$ has lower index $c_{j+1}$: the same position in both.
\item If $p\in\nu'$ and $p\notin\mu$, then $\xi(p)$ is fixed in the
 endpoint of $\gamma$, since no label of $\gamma$ has lower index $p$;
 and in the endpoint of $c$ it is Out, because $\nu'$ is increasing:
 the label $(p,k-1)$ moves it to $k-1$, and the next label, if any,
 moves it to a position larger than $p$.
\item If $p=a_i$, the value $e_i$ is carried to position $k$ and then,
 if $i<m$, to $a_{i+1}$ by $\mu$. If $i=m$ or $a_{i+1}\notin\nu$, it
 stays there in both endpoints. Otherwise $a_{i+1}\in\nu'$, because a
 lower index of $\mu$ cannot occur in $\nu_{\leq l}$ (it would have
 $j\geq j_0>l$); then $a_i<a_{i+1}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:typeB}(2), the
 value $e_i$ is Out at $a_{i+1}$ in the endpoint of $\gamma$, and in
 the endpoint of $c$ it is Out as well, at position $k-1$ or at a
 position larger than $a_{i+1}$, again because $\nu'$ is increasing.
\end{itemize}
Since $\gamma$ is an $S$-prefix, every $p\in F'\cap\nu'$ with
$p\notin\mu$, whose value is fixed in the endpoint of $\gamma$,
belongs to $S$. Hence the status of every $\xi(p)$ in the endpoint of
$c$ is admissible for $S$: it is unchanged, or $p\in F'\setminus S$
and the value is not fixed, or $p\in S$ and the value is Out. So
$c\in\mathcal C(S)$, and $c\in\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$.
Finally $l_0(c)=l$: if $l\geq1$ then $c_l\in F'\setminus S$ and
$c_l\notin\mu$, and no $j>l$ qualifies, since $c_j\in F'\cap\nu'$ with
$c_j\notin\mu$ lies in $S$.

\emph{Step 3: every chain of type B in $\mathcal C(S)$ arises.} Let
$c=\mu\nu\in\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$, $l=l_0(c)$,
$\gamma=\mu\nu_{\leq l}$ and $\nu'=\nu_{>l}$. The sequence $\nu'$ is
increasing: a descent $c_j>c_{j+1}$ with $j>l$ has $c_j\in F'$ and
$c_j\notin\mu$ by condition~4, and $\xi(c_j)$ is Left in the endpoint
of $c$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:descent-left}, so $c_j\notin S$ by
$S$-admissibility; thus $c_j\in F'\setminus S$, $c_j\notin\mu$ and
$j>l_0(c)$, which is absurd. The word $\gamma(k-1,k)\tilde\nu'$ is
directed, because $Z_l(\mu,\nu)\neq0$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:types}. It
remains to see that $\gamma$ is an $S$-prefix. If $l\geq1$, then
$c_l\in F'\setminus S$ and $c_l\notin\mu$ by definition, and $\gamma$
is tame: this is clear if $c$ is tame, and if $c$ is wild then
$c_j\in\mu$ for all $j\geq j_0$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:typeB}(1)), so
$c_l\notin\mu$ forces $l<j_0$, whence the first $l$ edges of $\nu$ are
Bruhat and their lower indices lie outside $\mu$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(2)). The comparison of statuses in
Step~2 applies verbatim ($c$ is of type~B, $\nu'$ is increasing and
avoids $\nu_{\leq l}$, and $c_l\notin\mu$) and shows that the
endpoint of $\gamma$ satisfies the conditions for an $S$-prefix: the
only values whose status differs from that in the endpoint of $c$ are
$\xi(c_l)$, which is counted as Left and has $c_l\in F'\setminus S$,
and the values $\xi(p)$ with $p\in F'\cap\nu'$, $p\notin\mu$, which
are fixed in the endpoint of $\gamma$; these $p$ lie in $S$, for
otherwise the index of $p$ in $\nu$ would exceed $l_0(c)$.

\emph{Step 4.} The maps of Steps~2 and~3 are inverse to each other:
Step~2 recovers $l=l_0(c)$ from $c$, and Step~3 produces the pair
$(\mu\nu_{\leq l_0(c)},\nu_{>l_0(c)})$ whose word is $c$. The weights
were computed in Step~2, and $|\gamma|+|\nu'|=|c|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The two families for general $F$]\label{prop:families-general}
Let $S\subseteq F'$. For $c=\mu\nu\in\mathcal W_B(S)$ let $i_1$ be
the index with $c_n=a_{i_1}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:typeB}(1)) and put
\[
 \Phi(c)=\bigl((a_1,k)\cdots(a_{i_1-1},k),\
  \nu\,(a_{i_1+1},k-1)\cdots(a_m,k-1)\bigr).
\]
For $c'=\mu'\nu'\in\mathcal W_A(S)$, with
$\mu'=(a'_1,k)\cdots(a'_{m'},k)$, $\nu'=(c'_1,k-1)\cdots(c'_{n'},k-1)$
and $l^\ast=\min J(c')$, put
\[
 \Psi(c')=\bigl(\mu'\,(c'_{l^\ast},k)(c'_{l^\ast+1},k)\cdots(c'_{n'},k),\
  \nu'_{\leq l^\ast}\bigr).
\]
Then $\Phi\colon\mathcal W_B(S)\to\mathcal W_A(S)$ and
$\Psi\colon\mathcal W_A(S)\to\mathcal W_B(S)$ are mutually inverse
bijections, $|\Phi(c)|=|c|-1$, and
\[
 P[c]\,\G_\xi=E\,P[\Phi(c)]\,\G_\xi\qquad\text{for all }c\in\mathcal W_B(S).
\]
For increasing chains these are the maps of
Proposition~\ref{prop:families}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $c\in\mathcal W_B(S)$ and $(\mu',\nu')=\Phi(c)$, so that
$\mu'=(a_1,k)\cdots(a_{i_1-1},k)$ and $\nu'$ is $\nu$ followed by the
labels $(a_i,k-1)$, $i>i_1$, in the order of $\mu$. The labels of
$\nu'$ are distinct, because no $a_i$ with $i>i_1$ occurs in $\nu$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:typeB}(1)). Condition~4 holds for the word
$\mu'\nu'$: inside $\mu'$ and inside $\nu$ it holds as in $c$, where
only the weaker requirement $c_j\notin\mu'$ is now needed for a
descent of $\nu$; a descent $a_i>a_{i+1}$ with $i>i_1$ has
$a_i\in F'$, and $a_i$ is neither in $\mu'$ nor an earlier lower index
of $\nu'$; and at the junction, $c_n=a_{i_1}$ is neither in $\mu'$ nor
an earlier lower index of $\nu'$, and if $a_{i_1}>a_{i_1+1}$ then
$a_{i_1}\in F'$, since this is a descent of $\mu$. Now
\[
 P[c]=P[\nu]\,K_{a_m}\cdots K_{a_{i_1+1}}\,K_{a_{i_1}}\,P[\mu'],
\]
and the operators $K_{a_i}$, $i>i_1$, commute with every $L_{c_j}$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:ybe}(1), as $a_i\neq c_j$), so
\[
 P[c]\,\G_\xi=K_{a_m}\cdots K_{a_{i_1+1}}\,P[\nu]\,K_{a_{i_1}}\,P[\mu']\,\G_\xi
 =W_n(\mu',\nu') ,
\]
the word $\mu'(a_{i_1},k)\nu'_{\leq n}\tilde\nu'_{>n}$ of
$W_n(\mu',\nu')$ being $\mu'$, then $(c_n,k)$, then $\nu$, then the
labels $(a_i,k)$, $i>i_1$. Since $c$ is directed,
$W_n(\mu',\nu')\neq0$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:telescoping} gives
$Z_0(\mu',\nu')=0$ and
$E\,P[\mu'\nu']\G_\xi=Z_{|\nu'|}(\mu',\nu')=W_n(\mu',\nu')=P[c]\G_\xi$.
In particular $\Phi(c)$ is directed, hence a level-$(k-2)$ block
chain, and it is of type~A, hence wild. Its endpoint is
$\operatorname{end}(c)\,t_{k-1,k}$, and exchanging the values at the
positions $k-1$ and $k$ does not change the status of any value
$\xi(p)$ with $p\leq k-2$; so $\Phi(c)$ is $S$-admissible, and
$\Phi(c)\in\mathcal W_A(S)$. Clearly $|\Phi(c)|=|c|-1$.

Let $c'=\mu'\nu'\in\mathcal W_A(S)$, $l^\ast=\min J(c')$ and
$(\mu'',\nu'')=\Psi(c')$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:after-quantum},
$c'_j\notin\mu'$ for $j\geq l^\ast$, with
$\xi(c'_j)\in(c^\ast,e'_{m'})$, while $c'_j\in\mu'$ for
$j_0\leq j<l^\ast$ and $c'_j\notin\mu'$ for $j<j_0$, where $j_0$ and
$e'_{m'}=\xi(a'_{m'})$ refer to $c'$ (and $e'_0=k$). We first show that
$c'_{l^\ast}>a'_{m'}$ if $m'\geq1$. Suppose $c'_{l^\ast}<a'_{m'}$, so
that $a'_{m'}$ lies strictly between $c'_{l^\ast}$ and $k-1$ at the
$l^\ast$th label of $\nu'$, and apply Lemma~\ref{lem:shape}(3) to
$c'$. If $l^\ast=j_0$, every such position carries a value smaller than
$f'_{l^\ast}=\xi(c'_{l^\ast})<e'_{m'}$; but $a'_{m'}$ is not among
$c'_1,\ldots,c'_{j_0-1}$, so it carries $e'_{m'-1}>e'_{m'}$: a
contradiction. If $l^\ast>j_0$, then $c'_{l^\ast-1}=a'_i\in\mu'$ and
$f'_{l^\ast-1}=e'_{i-1}$, and no such position carries a value in
$(\xi(c'_{l^\ast}),e'_{i-1})$. If $i=m'$, then
$c'_{l^\ast-1}=a'_{m'}>c'_{l^\ast}$ is a descent of $\nu'$ at a lower
index of $\mu'$, which condition~4 forbids; so $i<m'$. If $a'_{m'}$ is
not among $c'_1,\ldots,c'_{l^\ast-1}$, it carries $e'_{m'-1}$, and
$\xi(c'_{l^\ast})<e'_{m'}<e'_{m'-1}<e'_{i-1}$: a contradiction. If
$a'_{m'}=c'_{j'}$ with $j'<l^\ast$, then $j'\geq j_0$ and, the indices
$i(\cdot)$ being increasing, $m'=i(j')\leq i(l^\ast-1)=i<m'$: a
contradiction.

Hence $\mu''\nu''$ satisfies condition~4: inside $\mu'$ there is
nothing new; the junction $a'_{m'}<c'_{l^\ast}$ is an ascent; a descent
$c'_j>c'_{j+1}$ with $j\geq l^\ast$ has $c'_j\in F'$, by condition~4
for $c'$, as $c'_j\notin\mu'$ is not a repeated index there; and
inside $\nu''=\nu'_{\leq l^\ast}$ a descent $c'_j>c'_{j+1}$, $j<l^\ast$,
has $c'_j\in F'$ and $c'_j\notin\mu'$, hence $c'_j\notin\mu''$ since
$c'_j$ is not among $c'_{l^\ast},\ldots,c'_{n'}$. The labels of
$\mu''\nu''$ are distinct. Now
\[
 P[\mu''\nu'']=P[\nu'_{\leq l^\ast}]\,K_{c'_{n'}}\cdots K_{c'_{l^\ast+1}}\,K_{c'_{l^\ast}}\,P[\mu']
 =K_{c'_{n'}}\cdots K_{c'_{l^\ast+1}}\,P[\nu'_{\leq l^\ast}]\,K_{c'_{l^\ast}}\,P[\mu'] ,
\]
by Lemma~\ref{lem:ybe}(1), and the right side applied to $\G_\xi$ is
$W_{l^\ast}(\mu',\nu')$, which equals $E\,P[c']\G_\xi\neq0$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:types}. So $\Psi(c')$ is directed, hence a level-$(k-2)$
block chain, with $P[\Psi(c')]\G_\xi=E\,P[c']\G_\xi$; it is wild,
because $\nu''$ reuses the lower index $c'_{l^\ast}$ of $\mu''$; it is
$S$-admissible, its endpoint being
$\operatorname{end}(c')\,t_{k-1,k}$; and it is of type~B: the last
value of $\mu''$ is $e''_{m''}=\xi(c'_{n'})$, and a label $c'_j$ of
$\nu''$ with $c'_j\notin\mu''$ has $j<l^\ast$ and $c'_j\notin\mu'$,
hence $j<j_0$ and $\xi(c'_j)=f'_j<c^\ast$, so $j\notin J(\Psi(c'))$.
Thus $\Psi(c')\in\mathcal W_B(S)$.

The two maps are inverse to each other. For $c'\in\mathcal W_A(S)$,
the last label of $\nu''$ is $c'_{l^\ast}$, which is the $(m'+1)$th
lower index of $\mu''$, so $\Phi(\Psi(c'))=(\mu',\nu'_{\leq l^\ast}\nu'_{>l^\ast})=c'$.
For $c\in\mathcal W_B(S)$ with $(\mu',\nu')=\Phi(c)$, we determine
$J(\Phi(c))$, which is computed with $e'_{m'}=e_{i_1-1}$: a label
$c'_j$ of $\nu'$ with $j\leq n$ is $c_j$, which for $j<j_0$ has
$\xi(c_j)=f_j<c^\ast$, and for $j\geq j_0$ is $a_{i(j)}$ with
$i(j)\leq i_1$, so that $c_j\notin\mu'$ only for $j=n$, where
$\xi(a_{i_1})=e_{i_1}\in(c^\ast,e_{i_1-1})$. Hence $\min J(\Phi(c))=n$,
and $\Psi(\Phi(c))=\bigl(\mu'(a_{i_1},k)(a_{i_1+1},k)\cdots(a_m,k),\ \nu'_{\leq n}\bigr)=(\mu,\nu)=c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The block identity]\label{thm:block}
The block identity \eqref{eq:block} holds for every $k\geq3$, every
$\xi$ and every $F$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $k-1\notin F$ this is Proposition~\ref{prop:block-notfresh}. Let
$k-1\in F$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:chain-form} and the discussion
preceding \eqref{eq:chain-form-S}, it suffices to prove
\eqref{eq:chain-form-S} for every $S\subseteq F'$, in the clean frame.
Write $\Sigma(\mathcal X)=\sum_{c\in\mathcal X}\beta^{|c|}P[c]\G_\xi$
for a set $\mathcal X$ of chains. Then
$\mathcal P^0_S=\Sigma(\mathcal C(S))$ and
$\Pi_t\mathcal P^0_S=\Sigma(\mathcal T(S))$, so the left side of
\eqref{eq:chain-form-S} is
\[
 E\,\Sigma(\mathcal T(S))+\beta q_{k-1}\bigl(\Sigma(\mathcal T(S))+\Sigma(\mathcal W_A(S))+\Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))\bigr).
\]
By Proposition~\ref{prop:right-side} and $E^2=q_{k-1}$, the right side
is
\[
 (1+\beta E)E\bigl(\Sigma(\mathcal T(S))+\Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))\bigr)
 =(E+\beta q_{k-1})\bigl(\Sigma(\mathcal T(S))+\Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))\bigr).
\]
The difference of the two sides is
$E\,\Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))-\beta q_{k-1}\Sigma(\mathcal W_A(S))$. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:families-general},
\[
 \Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))=\sum_{c\in\mathcal W_B(S)}\beta^{|c|}E\,P[\Phi(c)]\G_\xi
 =\beta E\sum_{c\in\mathcal W_B(S)}\beta^{|\Phi(c)|}P[\Phi(c)]\G_\xi
 =\beta E\,\Sigma(\mathcal W_A(S)),
\]
so $E\,\Sigma(\mathcal W_B(S))=\beta E^2\Sigma(\mathcal W_A(S))=\beta q_{k-1}\Sigma(\mathcal W_A(S))$,
and the difference vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:main}
The backtrack identity \eqref{eq:backtrack} holds for every
$u\in S_\infty$ and every $k\geq2$. Consequently
Theorem~\ref{prop:top} holds for all $u$ and $k$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $k=2$ the backtrack identity is
Proposition~\ref{prop:backtrack-known}(i). For $k\geq3$ it follows
from Theorem~\ref{thm:block} by Proposition~\ref{prop:block-reduction}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:backtrack-implies} then gives Theorem~\ref{prop:top}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The proof matches the two sides of \eqref{eq:chain-form-S} term by
term: the terms $E\,P[c]\G_\xi$, $c\in\mathcal T(S)$, and
$\beta q_{k-1}P[c]\G_\xi$, $c\in\mathcal T(S)\sqcup\mathcal W_B(S)$,
occur on both sides, and the terms $\beta q_{k-1}P[c']\G_\xi$,
$c'\in\mathcal W_A(S)$, of the left side are matched with the terms
$E\,P[c]\G_\xi$, $c\in\mathcal W_B(S)$, of the right side through
$\Phi$. We have checked the identities
\eqref{eq:chain-form-S}, and the statements of
Lemmas~\ref{lem:after-quantum}--\ref{lem:typeB} and
Propositions~\ref{prop:right-side}--\ref{prop:families-general}, by
computer for every $\xi\in S_k$, every $F\ni k-1$ and every
$S\subseteq F'$ with $k\leq7$.
\end{remark}

\section{A small example and specializations}

\begin{example}\label{ex:s1}
Take $u=21$ and $k=1$. The endpoints are $21,312,132$, and the
identity permutation $e$. Their chain data $(s,D)$ are respectively
\[
 (0,0),\qquad(1,0),\qquad(2,(1,0,\ldots)),\qquad
 (1,(1,0,\ldots)).
\]
For instance, the path to $132$ is
\[
 213\xrightarrow{(1,3)}312\xrightarrow{(1,2)}132,
\]
whose second edge is quantum. Since
$B_1^q=x_1+z-\beta q_1(1+\beta x_1)$,
the formula reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:example}
\begin{split}
 \bigl(x_1+z-\beta q_1(1+\beta x_1)\bigr)\G_{21}^q
 ={}&
 \left(z-\frac{y_2}{1+\beta y_2}\right)\G_{21}^q\\
 &+\frac{\G_{312}^q+\beta q_1\G_{132}^q+q_1}
              {1+\beta y_2}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $\G_e^q=1$, and all polynomials have alphabets $(x;y)$.
This particular identity can be verified by expanding the four
quantum double Grothendieck polynomials and clearing the denominator.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Classical specialization.}
When all $q_i=0$, only paths without quantum edges contribute.
The multiplier becomes $\prod_{i=1}^k(x_i+z)$, giving the
classical double Grothendieck top-block formula as a specialization
of Theorem~\ref{prop:top}; this specialization is proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:classical}. The equivariant classical formula is
itself new, rather than an appeal to a previously published top-block
formula.

\paragraph{Non-equivariant specialization.}
When all $y_a=0$, the coefficient becomes
\[
 q^D\beta^{s-m}z^{|\Fix|}(1-\beta z)^{|\Left|}.
\]
In addition setting $z=0$ leaves only endpoints with $\Fix=\varnothing$,
and the surviving coefficient is $q^D\beta^{s-k}$.
At $\beta=-1$ it is $(-1)^{s-k}q^D$.
This specialization is Theorem~\ref{thm:noneq}, derived from the quantum
Grothendieck Pieri formula of Naito--Sagaki \cite{naito2024pieri}
through the uniqueness of chains and Lemma~\ref{lem:h-left}.

\paragraph{Cohomological specialization.}
At $\beta=0$, terms with $s>m$ vanish. Those with $s=m$ have coefficient
\[
 q^D\prod_{a\in\Fix}(z-y_a).
\]
The corresponding comparison is with the top quantum double
Schubert block in coefficient alphabet $-y$.

\paragraph{Usual K-theory variables.}
At $\beta=-1$ the multiplicative equivariant variables in these
conventions may be written
\[
 Y_a=\frac1{1-y_a}.
\]
Thus comparisons with formulas in the $Y$ variables use
$y_a=1-Y_a^{-1}$. This change of variables does not alter the
permutation indices or the quantum parameters $q_i$.
